Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Translation Background••
	• Based on Johannes Ficker's critical edition of Luther's Romerbriefvorlesung (1938)
	• Ficker's edition is part of the Weimar Edition of Luther's Works (Vol. 56)
• ••Luther's Commentary Structure••
	• Consists of two parts: the Gloss and the Scholia
	• The Scholia is translated in its entirety, while the Gloss is not (only glosses referred to in the Scholia are translated)
• ••Glosses and Interlinear Notes••
	• Not all glosses are translated, only those that are important or have significant interpretations
	• Glosses on Rom. 8:18-30 are translated in full to demonstrate Luther's method of interpretation
• ••Contextualization of Luther's Comments••
	• The entire passage of the letter is quoted in boldface type to show Luther's comments in context
• ••Bibliographic References••
	• Ficker identifies all Luther's references to the Bible and other literature
	• The translator follows Ficker's work and provides explanatory notes
• ••Luther's Use of the Vulgate••
	• Luther used the Vulgate translation of the Bible
	• The translator approximates the words and mannerisms of the Vulgate in the translation
• ••Biblical Quotations••
	• The translator relies on the Douay and Rheims edition of the Latin Vulgate
	• Quotations are not translated slavishly, but rather to reflect Luther's usage
• ••Biblical Book Names••
	• The translator follows the Protestant tradition in naming Biblical books
	• Adaptations are made for the book of Psalms
• ••Editorial Approach••
	• Ficker's work is relied upon to identify sources and provide explanations
	• The translator provides explanatory notes and references to Ficker's edition
• ••Translation Goals••
	• To provide a representative sample of Luther's method of Biblical interpretation
	• To enable readers to see Luther's comments in relation to the entire Pauline letter

• ••Author's Approach••
	• The author has followed Ficker's work for the most part, but not entirely
	• The author has indicated the sources in the footnotes
	• A list of works appears in the bibliography

	• The author quotes the fathers and Scholastics in the conventional way
	• The section referred to by Luther can be easily identified in any edition or translation
	• Commentaries are referenced in connection with the exegesis of the Biblical passage
• ••Modern Works••
	• The author occasionally directs the reader's attention to modern works of Luther interpretation
	• These discussions are especially enlightening, in the author's judgment
	• A full list of books, essays, and articles dealing with Luther's lectures on Romans is given at the end of the book
• ••Translations••
	• There is a German translation of Luther's lectures by Eduard Ellwein
	• The author has carefully compared this translation with their own
	• The author has benefited from this comparison
• ••English Translation••
	• There is an English translation of Luther's lectures by John Theodore Mueller
	• Mueller calls his work a "digest" of Luther's lectures
	• He translates most of the marginal gloss but omits the major part of the Scholia
• ••Other English Works••
	• A few recent English or American works contain translations of key passages of Luther's commentary
	• Prof. E. G. Rupp's work is especially notable
	• The author has gratefully adopted some of Rupp's phrasings
• ••Luther's Lectures on Romans••
	• Luther's exposition of the letter to the Romans is considered a work of genius
	• It is regarded as one of the most important works of the Reformer
	• The lectures were published for the first time in 1908
• ••Importance of the Lectures••
	• The lectures show that the basic ideas of the Protestant Reformation were formed in Luther's mind before he began his career as a reformer
	• They furnish clear and impressive proof of Luther's spiritual and theological maturity
	• They demonstrate why Luther could not be forced to recant his views
• ••Luther's Career••
	• Luther was thirty-four years old when he wrote the lectures
	• He was spiritually and theologically mature and resourceful
	• He was able to begin the Reformation with confidence and conviction
• ••Conclusion••
	• The author's work is a thorough and careful treatment of Luther's lectures on Romans
	• The author has drawn on a wide range of sources and has benefited from the work of other scholars
	• The lectures are a crucial part of Luther's theological development and a key to understanding the Protestant Reformation.

â€¢ ••Luther's Manuscript on Romans••
	• Written for personal use, not for publication
	• Shows Luther's thought process and preparation for teaching
	• Carefully prepared, with a final clean copy prepared from notes

â€¢ ••The Manuscript's History••
	• Written in 1515-1516, during Luther's lectures on Romans
	• Used as a guide for his students' notes
	• Still exists today, with several sets of notes located by Prof. Johannes Ficker

â€¢ ••Luther's Teaching Style••
	• Dictated to students, with careful pronunciation and repetition of difficult words
	• Used his manuscript as a guide, but abbreviated theological exposition
	• Left out sections critical of the church and its leaders

â€¢ ••The Manuscript's Content••
	• Contains Luther's thoughts on the gospel and his understanding of Paul's letter to the Romans
	• Shows Luther's struggle for clarity and understanding of the gospel
	• Criticizes the church and its leaders, but these sections are often left out

â€¢ ••Luther's Background••
	• Became a doctor of theology in 1512, allowing him to assume a chair at the University of Wittenberg
	• Had previously taught at the University of Erfurt and the University of Wittenberg
	• Had a background in Aristotelian philosophy and theology

â€¢ ••Luther's Teaching Experience••
	• Taught The Psalms from 1513-1515, before moving on to Romans
	• Used his manuscript as a guide for his students' notes
	• Dictated to students, with careful attention to pronunciation and repetition

	• Includes several sets of notes from Luther's lectures on Romans

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's Lectures on Romans••
	• Were found in the 19th century and published in 1885 as part of the Weimar Edition
	• Show Luther's solid scholarship and creative originality
• ••Preparation of the Lectures••
	• Luther had the printer Johann Grunenberg print the text of The Psalms with large spaces between lines and wide margins
	• Luther entered interlinear and marginal glosses in his own copy of the text
	• He dictated the glosses to students, who had printed copies of the Biblical text
• ••Printing and Textual Notes••
	• Grunenberg printed the text of Romans in a way that allowed for glosses to be inserted
	• Luther followed the Vulgate according to the Basel Edition of 1509, with some corrections
	• He used Roman type, preferred by Humanists
• ••Lecture Schedule••
	• Luther met with students twice a week, Mondays and Fridays, at 6 A.M.
	• He needed three semesters to complete the course on Romans
• ••Course Structure••
	• Luther dealt with Rom. 1:1 to 3:4 in the first semester
	• He dealt with chs. 3:5 to 8:39 in the second semester
	• He dealt with chs. 9:1 to 16:27 in the third semester
• ••Later Lectures and Publications••
	• Luther lectured on the letter to the Galatians in 1516-1517 and published a commentary in 1519
	• He published a commentary on The Psalms in 1519-1520
	• He dealt repeatedly with individual psalms and published his interpretations of many of them
• ••Teaching Career••
	• Luther remained a professor of Biblical theology until the end of his life
	• He devoted his academic lectures to Biblical exegesis throughout his career
• ••Publication of Commentaries••
	• Luther's commentaries were based on his lectures and used his earlier notes
	• He published commentaries on The Psalms, the letter to the Galatians, and The Letter to the Hebrews
• ••Later Teaching and Publications••
	• Luther lectured on the letter to the Galatians again in 1531
	• He published his interpretations of many individual psalms
• ••Legacy••
	• Luther's lectures and commentaries remain an important part of his legacy as a theologian and scholar

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's Lectures on Romans••
	• Luther likely lectured on Romans in 1515-1516
	• A student's notebook of his lectures has survived
• ••The Manuscript's History••
	• The manuscript was bound in 1582 by Paul Luther, Luther's son
	• It was seen by Johann Wiegand, who handled and examined Luther's autographs
• ••The Manuscript's Ownership••
	• The manuscript was owned by Luther's heirs, including his son Paul
	• It was sold to Joachim Frederick, Margrave of Brandenburg in 1594
• ••The Manuscript's Discovery••
	• The manuscript was deposited in the Royal Library of Berlin (now the Prussian State Library)
	• It was listed in the library's oldest printed catalogue (1668)
• ••The Manuscript's Display••
	• The manuscript was prominently displayed in a special exhibit in 1846
	• It was placed in a showcase in the library's entrance hall
• ••The Manuscript's Importance••
	• The manuscript was identified as an early Luther manuscript, but its importance was not recognized
	• The librarian Valentin Rose expressed surprise that no scholar had examined it
• ••The Manuscript's Publication••
	• The manuscript was finally published by Prof. Hans von Schubert in 1916
	• A final edition was prepared by K. A. Meissinger for the Weimar edition (Vol. 57, Gal.)
• ••The Manuscript's Significance••
	• The manuscript provides valuable insight into Luther's early thought and teaching
	• It is a rare example of Luther's autographs and early writings
• ••The Manuscript's Preservation••
	• The manuscript was preserved carefully by Luther's heirs
	• It was treasured highly by his family and later by the library
• ••The Manuscript's Legacy••
	• The manuscript has been recognized as an important historical document
	• It has been studied and published by scholars, shedding new light on Luther's thought and teaching.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Search for Luther's Manuscripts••
	• In 1905, a search was underway to find a specific piece of writing by Luther.
	• A copy of the writing was found in the Vatican Library, attributed to Johann Aurifaber.
• ••Johann Aurifaber's Role••
	• Aurifaber was one of Luther's faithful famuli (assistants).
	• He made copies of Luther's manuscripts, including lectures on Romans, at the request of Ulrich Fugger.
• ••Ulrich Fugger's Collection••
	• Fugger was a collector of rare books and manuscripts.
	• He was a Protestant who wanted to include Luther's original writings in his collection.
	• When he couldn't acquire the originals, he commissioned Aurifaber to make copies.
• ••The Fugger Library••
	• The library was left to the elector Palatine in Fugger's will.
	• It was deposited in the Church of the Holy Spirit in Heidelberg.
• ••The Transfer of the Fugger Library••
	• After the Thirty Years' War, the Upper Palatinate came under the control of Maximilian I, Duke of Bavaria.
	• Maximilian gave the Fugger Library to Pope Gregory XV as a token of his loyalty.
• ••The Vatican Library••
	• The Fugger Library was transported to Rome and incorporated into the Vatican Library.
	• The library became one of the most valuable in Germany, with 3,527 manuscripts.
• ••The Discovery of Luther's Manuscripts••
	• In the late 19th century, researchers searched the Vatican Library for manuscripts.
	• Dr. Hermann Vopel found a copy of Aurifaber's manuscript of Luther's lectures on Romans.
• ••The Significance of the Discovery••
	• The discovery of the manuscripts demonstrated the importance of a fuller knowledge of Luther's early works.
	• The publication of Heinrich Denifle's work in 1904 sparked a heated debate about Luther's theology.
• ••The Search for the Original Manuscript••
	• Prof. Johannes Ficker searched for the original manuscript but was only able to find students' notebooks.
	• The Royal Library in Berlin replied negatively to a questionnaire about the manuscript's location.
• ••The Legacy of the Discovery••
	• The discovery of the manuscripts has contributed significantly to our understanding of Luther's theology and early works.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Importance of Luther's Early Exegetical Lectures••
	• A detailed examination of Luther's theological development was necessary for understanding the Reformation
	• Publishing Luther's early exegetical lectures was crucial for a proper understanding of the Reformation's beginnings

â€¢ ••Discovery of Luther's Original Manuscript••
	• The existence of Luther's original manuscript in the Berlin library was brought to the attention of Prof. Nikolaus Miiller in 1905
	• Miiller claimed the right to publish the manuscript, but Prof. Ficker eventually gained access to it

â€¢ ••Publication of Luther's Lectures on Romans••
	• Prof. Ficker published a provisional version of the lectures in 1908
	• The definitive edition was published in 1938 as Vol. 56 of the Weimar Edition of Luther's Works

â€¢ ••Luther's Dependence on Medieval Exegesis••
	• Luther relied on the methods and traditions of medieval exegesis in his lectures
	• However, he was not a mere traditionalist and sought to understand the Scripture as thoroughly as possible

â€¢ ••Luther's Use of Exegetical Helps••
	• He employed the methods of traditional Biblical exegesis as well as recent Biblical research
	• He relied on Scholasticism and Humanism, but ultimately depended on his own insights and conscience

â€¢ ••Luther's New Phase in Exegesis••
	• Luther's lectures on Romans inaugurated a new phase in the history of exegesis
	• He combined the purposes of Scholastic exegesis and Humanism through a profound spiritual effort

â€¢ ••Luther's Use of Modern Exegetical Helps••
	• He used Reuchlin's Hebrew Dictionary, Faber's Biblical commentaries, and Erasmus' edition of the Greek New Testament
	• He also employed Erasmus' "Annotations" in his lectures

â€¢ ••Luther's Combination of Exegetical Approaches••
	• He combined the purposes of Scholastic exegesis (spiritual meaning) with those of Humanism (authorial intent)
	• He accomplished this through a profound spiritual effort that engaged all his inner powers

â€¢ ••Luther's New Understanding of the Bible••
	• Luther's lectures on Romans show that he was on the way to a new understanding of the Bible
	• This new understanding was connected to a fresh hearing of the gospel, which would eventually lead to the Reformation

â€¢ ••Luther's Break with Medieval Exegesis••
	• Luther's lectures on Romans demonstrate his break with medieval exegesis
	• He moved beyond the "gloss" method of medieval exegetes, which focused on individual words and terms.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Medieval Exegesis••
	• Characterized by a focus on details and a lack of understanding of the larger meaning of passages
	• Often got stuck in details, similar to many modern Biblical exegetes

â€¢ ••Types of Glosses••
	• Interlinear gloss: a paraphrase of the text, stressing the specific meaning of words and logical connections
	• Marginal gloss: brief interpretations of small units of the text, often excerpts from established works with added remarks

â€¢ ••Scholion (Scholium)••
	• An exposition of parts of the text that require extensive interpretation
	• Often used for practical exegesis, controversies, and arguments with opponents

â€¢ ••Distinctive Feature of Medieval Exegesis••
	• Orientation towards auctoritas and auctoritates (authority of canonical Scriptures, creeds, councils, etc.)

â€¢ ••Role of Authority in Exegesis••
	• Before advancing their own interpretations, Schoolmen quoted and exploited basic works representing traditional authorities

â€¢ ••Walafrid Strabo's Glossa Ordinaria••
	• A universal commentary on Scripture, interpreting it word for word or sentence by sentence
	• Built on extracts from the works of the fathers, such as Ambrose, Augustine, and Jerome

â€¢ ••Glossa Ordinaria and Glossa Interlinearis••
	• Commonly copied and printed together
	• The Glossa Interlinearis was a translation of the words and terms of the Bible into their inner meaning

â€¢ ••Luther's Early Commentaries••
	• Consisted of interlinear and marginal glosses and scholia
	• Frequently referred to the Glossa Ordinaria and Glossa Interlinearis

â€¢ ••Luther's Development as an Exegete••
	• Initially closely bound to the established manner of interpretation
	• Gradually freed himself from it, abandoning it altogether from 1519 on

â€¢ ••Luther's Intellectual Work••
	• Characterized by a succinct restatement of words in the interlinear gloss
	• Exhibited a characteristic way of doing intellectual work in his lectures on Romans

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Exegetical Method••
	• He explains individual passages by referencing other parts of the letter
	• He formulates comments and paraphrases to exclude meanings not intended by the apostle

â€¢ ••Luther's Marginal Glosses••
	• No longer mere collectanea from the writings of the fathers
	• Brief, incisive comments on selected passages or terms
	• Quotations from Scripture, critical or commendatory remarks on the writings of certain fathers (e.g. Jerome, Augustine)

â€¢ ••Luther's Scholia••
	• Short or lengthy essays on theological ideas or monastic/ecclesiastical practices
	• Often highly impassioned and personal, but less so in dictated notes to students

â€¢ ••The Scholastic Interpretation of the Bible••
	• Required both literal and spiritual exposition
	• Based on the hermeneutical practices of the ancient Greeks (e.g. allegorical interpretation of Homer's poems)

â€¢ ••The Four Senses of Scripture••
	• Historical sense: words signify things
	• Allegorical sense: things signify other things
	• Tropological sense: interpretation of matters affecting Christ and our obligation
	• Anagogical sense: interpretation of eternal glory

â€¢ ••Thomas Aquinas' Definition of the Four Senses••
	• God is the author of Holy Scripture, giving meaning to words and things
	• The four senses are: historical, allegorical, tropological, and anagogical

â€¢ ••Guibert de Nogent's Description of the Four Senses••
	• Every page of Scripture turns on four wheels: history, allegory, tropology, and anagoge

â€¢ ••Luther's Quotation of a Verse on the Four Senses••
	• "Littera gesta docet; quid credas allegoria; / Moralis quid agas; sed quid speres anagoge" (Letter teaches history, allegory teaches what to believe, morality teaches what to do, anagoge teaches what to hope for)

â€¢ ••The Complexity of the Scholastic Interpretation of the Bible••
	• Required several hermeneutical steps to achieve spiritual interpretation
	• The allegorical meaning was further defined over time

â€¢ ••Luther's Engagement with Tradition••
	• He was deeply entangled in the Scholastic interpretation of the Bible
	• He quotes and references the writings of earlier scholars (e.g. Origen, Jerome, Augustine)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Fourfold Method of Interpretation••
	• A medieval approach to biblical interpretation that involves four levels of meaning: literal, allegorical, moral, and anagogical.
	• This method was used by Martin Luther in his lectures on Galatians.

â€¢ ••The Four Levels of Meaning••
	• ••Literal••: the historical or factual meaning of a passage.
	• ••Allegorical••: the symbolic or spiritual meaning of a passage.
	• ••Moral••: the moral or practical application of a passage.
	• ••Anagogical••: the mystical or spiritual meaning of a passage, often related to heaven or the afterlife.

â€¢ ••Luther's Use of the Fourfold Method••
	• Luther used this method in his lectures on Galatians, illustrating it with examples from the Bible.
	• He saw the four levels of meaning as interconnected and mutually informing.

â€¢ ••E. G. Rupp's Assessment of the Fourfold Method••
	• Rupp suggests that this method provides a more adequate framework for understanding the Bible than might initially be supposed.
	• He sees the four levels of meaning as linked to the Christian proclamation and the experience of God's salvation.

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Medieval Exegesis••
	• Despite the potential of the fourfold method, medieval exegesis often conformed to established patterns and themes.
	• Interpreters often focused on matters not mentioned in the text, and the method was not always used in a nuanced or thoughtful way.

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Allegorical Interpretation••
	• Luther came to see the allegorical method as a way of distorting the Scripture.
	• He was critical of Origen, whom he saw as the father of allegorical exegesis.

â€¢ ••Luther's Appreciation of Nicholas of Lyra••
	• Luther learned to appreciate the work of Nicholas of Lyra, a Franciscan and doctor of the Sorbonne.
	• Lyra's commentary, the Postillae perpetuae, was seen as a supplement to the glosses of Walafrid Strabo and Anselm of Laon.

â€¢ ••Lyra's Approach to Biblical Interpretation••
	• Lyra, a converted Jew, had little use for the "spiritual" interpretation of the Bible.
	• He valued a more literal and historical approach to biblical interpretation.

â€¢ ••The Influence of the Fourfold Method on Luther's Thought••
	• Despite its limitations, the fourfold method had a significant influence on Luther's thought and biblical interpretation.
	• Luther's use of the method helped shape his understanding of the Bible and his approach to Christian theology.

â€¢ ••The Legacy of the Fourfold Method••
	• The fourfold method of interpretation has had a lasting impact on Christian theology and biblical interpretation.
	• Its influence can still be seen in modern ecumenical theology and biblical scholarship.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Exegetical Influences••
	• Luther was influenced by Jewish commentaries on the Old Testament, particularly Rabbi Solomon Rashi's work.
	• He emphasized the importance of literal or historical interpretation.

â€¢ ••Publication of Bibles••
	• John Froben's publication of Bibles in 1498 and 1509 included the interlinear gloss of Anselm, the Glossa ordinaria, and the Postil of Nicholas of Lyra.
	• This publication was a valuable resource for Luther.

â€¢ ••Luther's Relationship with Lyra••
	• Luther initially disliked Lyra's work but later grew fond of it.
	• Lyra's emphasis on the literal sense was unusual among Scholastic exegetes.

â€¢ ••Scholastic Exegesis••
	• Scholastic exegetes regarded the literal sense as the basis of all interpretation.
	• They believed that spiritual meaning should not be given to a passage unless it was clearly indicated in Scripture.

â€¢ ••Thomas Aquinas' Rule••
	• Aquinas stated that nothing can be subsumed under the spiritual sense unless it is clearly indicated in Scripture through its literal meaning.

â€¢ ••Faber Stapulensis' Influence••
	• Luther was influenced by Faber's views, particularly in his Dictata super Psalterium.
	• Faber's works, including his commentary on the Pauline letters, were important references for Luther.

â€¢ ••Faber's Exegetical Principle••
	• Faber proposed that the interpreter must search for the literal sense intended by the Holy Spirit.
	• He distinguished between two literal senses: historical-literal and prophetic-literal.

â€¢ ••Faber's View of Literal Sense••
	• Faber believed that the historical-literal sense was improper because it was the product of human imagination.
	• He regarded the prophetic-literal sense as proper because it was inspired by the Holy Spirit.

â€¢ ••Luther's Exegetical Approach••
	• Luther attempted to interpret the Bible in a literal-grammatical way, assuming a prophetic-spiritual meaning.
	• He believed that the Holy Spirit led the exegete to this understanding.

â€¢ ••Luther's Goals••
	• Luther aimed to avoid the historicism of Lyra and the hermeneutical fancifulness of the allegorists.
	• He sought to establish an accurate grammatical understanding of the Hebrew and Greek texts.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Approach to Biblical Interpretation••
	• He aimed to balance literal-historical interpretation with spiritual meaning
	• He sought to demonstrate the spiritual significance of the Bible

â€¢ ••Influence of Neoplatonic Mystics••
	• He was influenced by Neoplatonic mystics, including Dionysius Areopagitica and Nicholas of Cusa
	• This influenced his Christocentric interpretation of the Bible

â€¢ ••Contrast between Spirit and Letter••
	• Luther used the contrast between spirit and letter to interpret the Bible
	• He saw the Bible as having a dualistic nature, with spiritual and carnal aspects

â€¢ ••Christocentric Emphasis••
	• Luther's Christocentric emphasis was strong in his lectures on Romans
	• He believed that the Bible should be understood in its entirety with respect to Christ

â€¢ ••Marginal Gloss on Romans 1:1-2••
	• Luther wrote a marginal gloss on Paul's appeal to the gospel of God
	• He emphasized the importance of understanding the Bible in its entirety with respect to Christ

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Romans 10:6••
	• Luther interpreted the question of Moses, "Who shall ascend into heaven?" as a reference to Christ
	• He believed that the Bible, if contemplated inwardly, deals everywhere with Christ

â€¢ ••Christ as the End of the Law••
	• Luther believed that Christ is the end of the law (Rom. 10:4)
	• He saw every word of the Bible as pointing to Christ

â€¢ ••Development of Exegetical Method••
	• Luther developed an exegetical method that reflected his own insight and inner struggle
	• He combined the literal-prophetic meaning with the traditional fourfold meaning of the Bible

â€¢ ••Abandonment of Traditional Method••
	• Faber believed that the interpretation of the Scripture according to the sensus literalis propheticus superseded the Scholastic exegesis
	• Luther, however, handled the hermeneutical issue in a more complicated fashion

â€¢ ••Combination of Methods••
	• Luther made the literal-prophetic meaning the basis of the fourfold meaning
	• He combined the traditional method with his own Christocentric interpretation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Approach to Scripture••
	• Combined the quadriga with Faber's method
	• Focused on spiritual exegesis
	• Emphasized the connection between literal-prophetic and moral senses

â€¢ ••Influence of Humanism and Erasmus••
	• Studied Reuchlin and Erasmus' contributions to Biblical exegesis
	• Found Erasmus' scholarship unsatisfactory
	• Preferred Augustine's writings for understanding Scripture

â€¢ ••Development of Luther's Hermeneutic••
	• Combined literal-prophetic and moral senses
	• Focused on Christ as the central theme of Scripture
	• Emphasized the connection between Christ and believers

â€¢ ••Key Principles of Luther's Hermeneutic••
	• The Bible speaks literaliter spiritualiter (spiritual-literal meaning) of Christ
	• The Bible speaks tropologice (moral sense) of the believer in Christ
	• What is true of Christ is true of his disciples

â€¢ ••The Role of the Tropological Method••
	• Became the sensus primarius Scripturae (primary sense of Scripture)
	• Assumed that the Bible is the work of God and the Holy Spirit
	• Correlated with literal-Christological interpretation

â€¢ ••God's Word and Action••
	• God's works are his words
	• God's doing is identical with his speaking
	• Required a "causative interpretation" of Biblical statements about God

â€¢ ••Examples of Luther's Hermeneutic••
	• Interpreted "God's way" as the way God causes us to walk
	• Interpreted "God is holy" as God causing men to be holy

â€¢ ••Luther's Lectures on the Psalms••
	• Developed the tropological method further
	• Emphasized the connection between Christ and believers

â€¢ ••Assumptions of Luther's Hermeneutic••
	• The Bible is the work of God and the Holy Spirit
	• The Bible speaks of Christ as the central theme

â€¢ ••Key Implications of Luther's Hermeneutic••
	• Emphasized the connection between Christ and believers
	• Focused on the spiritual-literal meaning of Scripture
	• Required a "causative interpretation" of Biblical statements about God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's Key Insight••
	• Luther's understanding of the Scripture was centered on the concept of "the righteousness of God" (iustitia Dei)
	• Tropologically, this righteousness is understood as faith in Christ (fides Christi)
• ••Exegetical Insight••
	• Luther's insight was rooted in his exegesis of Romans 1:17
	• He understood the "righteousness of God" as revealed in the gospel from faith to faith
• ••The Righteousness of God••
	• God alone is righteous
	• Man is a sinner and nothing but a sinner, especially if he regards himself as righteous
	• Self-righteousness is presumption and pride, making man a liar before himself and God
• ••God's Justice and Mercy••
	• God sends grace through his justice
	• He reveals himself as righteous to make man righteous
	• He kills in order to make alive, breaks down pride to heal, condemns to forgive, and humiliates to accept in grace
• ••Luther's Interpretation of Romans••
	• Luther saw Romans as a letter against those who are self-righteous and confident in their works
	• The apostle leads them to take the grace of God seriously by acknowledging and confessing their sin
• ••The Importance of Acknowledging Sin••
	• Acknowledging sin is necessary to glorify God's mercy
	• Believing in one's own righteousness is pride and prevents glorifying God's mercy
• ••Luther's Discovery of the Gospel••
	• Luther's understanding of God's grace and man's sin was the discovery of the gospel
	• It gave him a sense of mission as a professor of Biblical theology
• ••Luther's Critique of the Church••
	• Luther believed that the theologians and churchmen of his time were bound to ways of thought and practice leading away from Christ
	• He felt he had rediscovered the gospel for Christendom and the church
• ••The Role of Faith••
	• Faith in Christ is the righteousness of God
	• Faith is necessary to take the grace of God seriously and acknowledge one's sin
• ••The Relationship between Sin and Grace••
	• Sin and falsehood abound to God's glory when humbled and confessed
	• God's mercy is glorified when sin is acknowledged and forgiven

â€¢ ••Luther's understanding of the gospel••
	• Led him to feel called to reform the church
	• Drove him to speak out against Scholastic theology, monastic religion, and ecclesiastical ceremonialism

â€¢ ••Luther's faith and its impact••
	• Led him to ask others to repent and change their minds
	• Led him to believe that the word of God comes to change and renew the world
	• Compelled him to advance fundamental criticisms against Roman Catholic beliefs and practices

â€¢ ••The Protestant Reformation••
	• Was the result of Luther's theology

	• Led to the formation of nonpapal, Reformed, Evangelical churches

â€¢ ••Luther's writings and their significance••
	• His preface to the first volume of the Wittenberg Edition of his Collected Latin Works (1545) is a key document
	• Explains the background and purpose of his writings from 1517-1521

â€¢ ••Luther's interpretation of the Psalms••
	• Was influenced by his lectures on Romans and Galatians
	• Was driven by a desire to understand Paul's writings
	• Was marked by a sense of urgency and ardor

â€¢ ••The role of Romans 1:17••
	• Was a key verse that stood in Luther's way
	• Was misunderstood by Luther as referring to formal or active justice
	• Was a turning point in Luther's understanding of the gospel

â€¢ ••Luther's personal struggles••
	• Felt himself to be a sinner with an unquiet conscience
	• Felt unable to be confident that he had pleased God
	• Hated the idea of a God who punished sinners

â€¢ ••Luther's relationship with God••
	• Felt angry with God for punishing sinners
	• Felt that God was a righteous God who punished sinners
	• Struggled with the idea of a God who was both just and merciful

â€¢ ••The impact of Luther's theology••
	• Led to a fundamental shift in Luther's understanding of the gospel

	• Influenced the development of the Protestant Reformation

	• Were a key part of Luther's theological development
	• Influenced his interpretation of the Psalms

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Initial Struggle with the Concept of God's Justice••
	• He was troubled by the idea of God's justice and the concept of original sin
	• He felt that God's justice was too harsh and that sinners were being unfairly punished

â€¢ ••The Turning Point: Understanding the Context of Romans 1:17••
	• Luther began to understand the context of Romans 1:17, which reads "The justice of God is revealed in it; as it is written, the just shall live by faith"
	• He realized that the justice of God refers to the gift of faith that makes the righteous man live

â€¢ ••The Revelation of God's Justice as Passive Justice••
	• Luther understood that the justice of God is not about punishing sinners, but about justifying them through faith
	• He saw that this justice is a gift from God, which makes us strong, wise, and saved

â€¢ ••The Significance of the Passage in Romans 1:17••
	• This passage became a "gate to Paradise" for Luther, revealing the true nature of God's justice
	• He saw that this passage was a key to understanding the entire Bible

â€¢ ••Luther's Interpretation of Augustine's On the Spirit and the Letter••
	• Luther read Augustine's work and found that he had interpreted the justice of God in a similar way
	• He was pleased to see that Augustine taught a justice of God that justifies us

â€¢ ••The Impact of Luther's Discovery on His Interpretation of Scripture••
	• After his discovery, Luther's interpretation of Scripture changed dramatically
	• He saw that many passages, including those in the Psalms, referred to the justice of God as a gift of faith

â€¢ ••Luther's Work on the Psalms and the Diet of Worms••
	• Luther began to work on a commentary on the Psalms, but was interrupted by the Diet of Worms
	• He was called to appear at the Diet, which was convened by Emperor Charles V

â€¢ ••The Significance of Luther's Work on Romans••
	• Luther's work on Romans was a crucial part of his discovery of the gospel and the doctrine of justification
	• His lectures on Romans show that he was already in possession of this understanding of justification

â€¢ ••The Astonishing Lack of Emphasis on Romans 1:17 in Luther's Lectures••
	• Despite the importance of Romans 1:17 to Luther, he only gave a brief explanation of it in his lectures
	• This is surprising, given the significance of this passage to his understanding of the gospel

â€¢ ••The Consistency of Luther's Understanding of Justification••
	• Throughout his commentary on Romans, Luther consistently shows an understanding of justification as a gift of faith
	• This understanding is consistent with his later statements about the significance of this discovery for his life and ministry

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Theological Heritage••
	• Influenced by the Bible, Augustine, and mystics like Bernard of Clairvaux and Tauler
	• Also influenced by Scholastics like Peter Lombard and Duns Scotus, and nominalists like Ockham and Gabriel Biel

â€¢ ••Biblical Influence••
	• Luther's theological outlook was determined by the Bible, particularly Paul's teaching
	• He read and pondered the Bible in the light of Paul's teaching

â€¢ ••Augustine's Influence••
	• Luther was familiar with Augustine's On the Spirit and the Letter
	• He was influenced by Augustine's theological ideas, particularly in his lectures on Romans

â€¢ ••Monastic Life and Training••
	• Luther's monastic life and training shaped his basic theological attitudes and concerns
	• He was steeped in the liturgical tradition and discipline of the Augustinian order

â€¢ ••Theological Schooling and Training••
	• Luther's theological schooling and training as a teacher had a deep impact on his mind
	• He was familiar with Aristotle's Ethics and Peter Lombard's writings

â€¢ ••Critical Thinking••
	• Luther was critical of Aristotle and the nominalists, despite being influenced by them
	• He was not afraid to express sharp criticisms of their ideas

â€¢ ••Source and Norm of Luther's Theology••
	• The Bible was the source and norm of all his agreements and disagreements with other writers
	• He believed that he had learned ignorance of sin, righteousness, and the Christian life from Scholastic theology, but had found Christ again in Paul

â€¢ ••Luther's Exegetical Method••
	• He developed his own exegetical method by critically engaging with the Biblical interpreters of former ages and his own time
	• He formed his own theological understanding of the Christian gospel by engaging in a searching conversation with other theologians

â€¢ ••Luther's Place in the History of Exegesis••
	• He was a key figure in the history of exegesis, introducing a new Biblical theology into Christendom
	• His lectures on Romans show that he was still feeling his way toward a theological position of his own

â€¢ ••Luther's Theological Position••
	• He was a man who was still feeling his way toward a theological position of his own
	• However, his writings and debates show that he was already a thinker who was to become the Reformer of the church.

Here are 10 bullets and sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's knowledge of the Bible••
	• He had an extraordinary knowledge of the Bible, which he said he had learned almost by heart through diligent study.
	• His knowledge was comparable to that of the best biblical scholars of antiquity, such as Tertullian and Origen.
• ••Luther's use of the Psalms••
	• He knew the Psalms intimately, not only through monastic devotions and worship services, but also through special study.
	• He tended to read the Psalter with the eyes of Paul and interpreted Paul through the piety of the Psalms.
• ••Luther's themes in the lectures on Romans••
	• He focused on God's dealing with man in righteousness and mercy, and man's standing before God as sinner or forgiven.
	• He frequently introduced special themes suggested by certain biblical passages.
• ••Luther's method of interpretation••
	• He often introduced themes that certain biblical passages suggested to him.
	• These themes continued to govern his thinking throughout his life.
• ••Luther's use of biblical passages••
	• He frequently quoted biblical passages to illustrate his points.
	• He often took biblical phrases out of context to express significant theological themes.
• ••Luther's interpretation of biblical passages••
	• He interpreted biblical passages in light of his own theological themes.
	• He saw connections between biblical passages that others might not have noticed.
• ••Luther's use of proverbs••
	• He was fond of proverbs and liked to quote them.
	• He saw proverbs as illustrations of universal truths about human nature.
• ••Luther's understanding of God's nature••
	• He described God's nature as one that "first destroys and turns to nothing whatever is in us before he gives us his own."
	• This understanding was derived from biblical passages such as Isaiah 28:21.
• ••Luther's understanding of God's work••
	• He described God's work as "strange" and "properly his own."
	• This understanding was derived from biblical passages such as Isaiah 28:21.
• ••Luther's mind and the Bible••
	• His mind was filled with the Bible, and biblical words formed his thoughts.
	• He often expressed his own thoughts through biblical words.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's use of the Bible••
	• He memorized the Bible and used its sayings in various connections
	• Examples include "the fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom" and "the sound of a rustling leaf shall terrify them"

â€¢ ••Luther's approach to the Bible••
	• He was thorough and meticulous in his interpretation of the Biblical books
	• He used various exegetical tools, including commentaries from Humanists

â€¢ ••Luther's use of Humanist commentaries••
	• He appreciated their philological and lexical work
	• However, he found their theological exposition of the Bible to be inadequate

â€¢ ••Luther's use of Erasmus' commentaries••
	• He quotes Erasmus frequently, but does not depend on him for theological ideas
	• Luther criticizes Erasmus for misunderstanding Paul's teachings on original sin and righteousness

â€¢ ••Luther's use of Augustine's commentaries••
	• He quotes Augustine more often than any other author in the lectures on Romans
	• He depends particularly on Augustine's anti-Pelagian writings

â€¢ ••Luther's familiarity with Augustine's work••
	• He is acquainted with almost the whole body of Augustine's work
	• He uses Augustine's Exposition of Certain Themes of the Letter to the Romans and Against Julian frequently

â€¢ ••Luther's use of other authors••
	• He quotes Cyprian and Chrysostom, but only through references to Augustine
	• He does not use On Predestination and On the Gift of Perseverance, despite being familiar with them

â€¢ ••Luther's dependence on other authors••
	• He is not dependent on Erasmus for theological ideas
	• He is slightly dependent on Faber, but not at all on Erasmus

â€¢ ••Luther's criticism of Erasmus••
	• He criticizes Erasmus for misunderstanding Paul's teachings on original sin and righteousness
	• He attributes Erasmus' failure to understand Paul's teachings to his lack of familiarity with Augustine's anti-Pelagian writings

â€¢ ••Luther's approach to Scripture••
	• He seeks to understand the apostle's thoughts and intentions in every word and sentence
	• He is not arbitrary in his use of the Bible, but rather thorough and meticulous in his interpretation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's relationship with Augustine••
	• Luther highly valued Augustine's writings and regarded him as a Paulinist
	• He saw Augustine as an ally in his fight against Pelagian tendencies in Scholasticism
• ••Luther's primary influence••
	• Luther was primarily a Paulinist, and only secondarily an Augustinian
	• He felt that Augustine's teachings were not always in line with the gospel
• ••Differences between Luther and Augustine••
	• Luther felt that Augustine did not reach the profundity of Paul's understanding of human nature
	• Luther disagreed with Augustine's teaching on love
• ••Understanding of sin••
	• Luther and Augustine agreed that sin consists of a basic proneness towards evil
	• Luther defined sin as defiance and self-righteousness, while Augustine saw it as pride and evil desiring
• ••Conception of salvation••
	• Luther criticized the Scholastics for believing that original sin and actual sins are canceled by sacramental grace
	• Luther believed that sin is forgiven by God, but remains until death
• ••Augustine's position••
	• Augustine believed that guilt and punishment of sin are forgiven, but concupiscence remains
	• Luther disagreed with Augustine's view of concupiscence as a spur to sin, seeing it as selfishness and sin itself
• ••Luther's view of human nature••
	• Luther saw humans as simultaneously sinful and righteous
	• He believed that Christians are sinners in fact, but righteous in hope
• ••Luther's doctrine of simul peccator ac iustus••
	• This doctrine holds that Christians are both sinners and righteous at the same time
	• Luther believed that this doctrine is a bold and necessary teaching of the gospel
• ••Luther's critique of Scholasticism••
	• Luther criticized the Scholastics for their view of sin and salvation
	• He saw them as failing to take full account of human perversity and evil
• ••Luther's relationship with Scholastic-Aristotelian teaching••
	• Luther disagreed with the Scholastics' view of sin as single acts of wrongdoing
	• He believed that sin is a fundamental aspect of human nature, and that every sin is a manifestation of original sin.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's View of Human Nature••
	• Luther believed that humans are both sinful and righteous at the same time
	• He was influenced by Augustine's writings, but also developed his own unique doctrine
• ••The Role of Christ's Righteousness••
	• Luther taught that Christ's righteousness "covers" and is "imputed" to the believer
	• This means that the believer is forgiven and righteous because of Christ, not because of their own actions
• ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Luther believed that sin remains even after forgiveness
	• He emphasized the weight of human sin and the need for ongoing healing and forgiveness
• ••The Relationship between Faith and Righteousness••
	• Luther taught that faith in Christ is the means by which we receive Christ's righteousness
	• This faith is not a work, but a gift that allows us to receive God's righteousness
• ••The Concept of "Foreign" Righteousness••
	• Luther believed that the righteousness we receive from Christ is a "foreign" righteousness, not our own
	• This means that our righteousness is not based on our own efforts, but on Christ's work on our behalf
• ••Luther's Use of Augustine's Writings••
	• Luther cited Augustine's writings at great length and with approval
	• However, he also developed his own unique doctrine that differed from Augustine's
• ••Luther's Relationship with Augustine••
	• Luther was influenced by Augustine's writings, but also felt free to disagree with him
	• He hesitated to express direct disagreement with Augustine, even when he strongly disagreed with him
• ••Luther's View of the Christian Life••
	• Luther believed that the Christian life is one of ongoing struggle and sin
	• However, he also believed that we are forgiven and righteous because of Christ's work on our behalf
• ••The Importance of Hope••
	• Luther emphasized the importance of hope in the Christian life
	• He believed that we are not yet fully healthy, but are being healed and will become healthy in the future
• ••The Danger of Presumption••
	• Luther warned against the danger of presumption, which can lead to a bad relapse into sin
	• He believed that we must always recognize our sinfulness and rely on Christ's righteousness for forgiveness and healing.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's View on Christian Love••
	• Luther believes Christian love (caritas) is never self-love
	• He argues that self-love can never be the pattern of loving one's neighbor

â€¢ ••Conflict with Augustine's View••
	• Augustine's definition of ordered love starts with loving God, then oneself, then one's neighbor, and finally one's body
	• Luther disagrees, saying that self-love is not a necessary step in loving one's neighbor

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Scholastic Theology••
	• Luther criticizes Scholastic theologians for failing to understand the nature of sin and grace
	• He believes they attribute too much potentiality to human nature and distort the gospel

â€¢ ••Luther's Exception for Augustine••
	• Luther excepts Augustine from his criticism of Scholastic theologians
	• He believes Augustine had a correct understanding of sin and grace, unlike the Scholastics

â€¢ ••Luther's Evaluation of Augustine••
	• Luther sees Augustine as a Scriptural theologian who understood the nature of sin and grace
	• He attributes Augustine's correct understanding to his reliance on Scripture, not Aristotle

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Scholastic Method••
	• Luther believes Scholastic theologians follow Aristotle's method in their Ethics
	• He argues that this method is flawed, as it bases sinfulness and righteousness on human actions

â€¢ ••Luther's View on Original Sin••
	• Luther believes original sin is not entirely taken away by baptism
	• He argues that sin (concupiscence) is forgiven in baptism, but still present

â€¢ ••Luther's Doctrine of Simul Iustus ac Peccator••
	• Luther teaches that the Christian is both justified and a sinner at the same time
	• He believes this doctrine is supported by Scripture and is in line with Augustine's teaching

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Scholastic Theologians••
	• Luther accuses Scholastic theologians of failing to "magnify" sin and its consequences
	• He believes this failure leads to a distorted understanding of the gospel and its message of liberty

â€¢ ••Luther's Importance of Recognizing Human Self-Seeking••
	• Luther believes it is essential to recognize human self-seeking in all its seriousness
	• He argues that this recognition is necessary for understanding the gospel and its message of liberty

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's struggle with sin••
	• Luther struggled to understand himself as a sinner despite receiving forgiveness through the sacrament of penance.
	• He realized that forgiveness is real, but sin is not taken away except in hope.
• ••Critique of Scholastic theologians••
	• Luther criticized Scholastic theologians, such as Ockham and Pierre d'Ailly, for saying that man can love God above everything by his own powers.
	• He argued that this view is "sheer madness" and that grace is necessary for fulfilling the law.
• ••The law and human nature••
	• Luther believed that the law cannot be fulfilled by human powers alone due to concupiscence.
	• He argued that man is unable to love God except if grace enables him to do so.
• ••The nature of sin and forgiveness••
	• Luther believed that the Scholastic theologians did not understand the nature of sin and forgiveness.
	• He argued that they were overly optimistic about human moral and religious potentialities.
• ••The role of good works••
	• Luther criticized the Scholastic theologians for focusing on good works rather than the nature of sin.
	• He argued that this leads to pride and a false sense of righteousness.
• ••The righteousness of faith••
	• Luther believed that the Christian life consists of the righteousness of faith, which is confidence in being freed from sin through God's action.
	• He argued that this is distinct from establishing righteousness through works.
• ••The Scholastic conception of law and grace••
	• Luther criticized the Scholastic theologians for saying that God has obliged us to have grace and therefore to fulfill the impossible.
	• He argued that this is an inept and foolish way of speaking.
• ••God's role in law and grace••
	• Luther believed that God has obliged us to fulfill the law in order to give us grace when we ask for it.
	• He argued that the Scholastic theologians have misrepresented God's role in law and grace.
• ••The burden of the law••
	• Luther argued that the Scholastic theologians have added an extra burden to the law by demanding that we possess God's grace.
	• He believed that this is a sign of pride and ignorance of God.
• ••The fulfillment of the law••
	• Luther discussed the Scholastic teaching on fulfilling the law "according to the substance of the deed" through an action done for the sake of God.
	• He argued that this view is flawed and that grace is necessary for fulfilling the law.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Luther's disagreement with the Scholastics••
	• Luther denies that man can keep the law
	• He asserts that man is justified by the imputation of Christ's righteousness
• ••The concept of "the elicited act of the will"••
	• The Scholastics (e.g. d'Ailly and Biel) identify "wanting to" with a small act of the will
	• Luther argues that this is not the same as the will to be righteous
• ••Augustine's quote and its interpretation••
	• Augustine said "Wanting to be righteous is a large part of righteousness"
	• Luther interprets this as meaning that wanting to be righteous is all the righteousness we can have in this life
	• The Scholastics interpret it as meaning that men have the power to become righteous if they want to
• ••Luther's understanding of Augustine••
	• Luther sees Augustine as an ally against the Scholastics
	• He appeals to Augustine's anti-Pelagian writings
• ••The Scholastics' understanding of Augustine••
	• They stress Augustine's ideas that reflect Neoplatonic humanism
	• They see man as able to ascend to God and to become righteous through the sacraments
• ••Luther's disagreement with the Scholastics' interpretation of Augustine••
	• Luther argues that the Scholastics misunderstand Augustine's quote
	• He believes that Augustine's quote refers to a longing that can never be fulfilled in this life
• ••Luther's reliance on Augustine••
	• Luther depends on Augustine's teachings in his fight against Pelagianism
	• He believes that he is in agreement with Augustine on the difference between the law and the gospel
• ••The Scholastic theologians opposed by Luther••
	• Luther rarely names specific authors, instead referring to "the Scholastic theologians" or "our theologians"
	• He quotes directly only two medieval theologians: Bernard of Clairvaux and Peter Lombard
• ••Luther's use of Augustine's writings••
	• He lists Augustine's anti-Pelagian writings and recommends that Erasmus read them
	• He quotes Augustine's Second Book Against Julian, where he discusses the concept of "to will"
• ••The significance of Luther's interpretation of Augustine••
	• Luther's interpretation of Augustine's quote reflects his understanding of the gospel and his rejection of Pelagianism
	• It highlights the differences between Luther's theology and that of the Scholastics.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's views on Scholasticism••
	• He was initially favorable to Scholasticism due to its Scriptural and Augustinian roots
	• He was particularly drawn to Bernard's mysticism and teaching on personal faith in forgiveness of sins

â€¢ ••Luther's criticism of Scholasticism••
	• He criticized the Scholastics for imitating saints and founders in an unspiritual way
	• He accused them of neglecting the spirit behind the writings of their founders

â€¢ ••Luther's views on specific Scholastics••
	• He specifically criticized Duns Scotus, but only once and in a sarcastic tone
	• He made no direct or indirect references to Thomas Aquinas, but may have alluded to him in a critique of the Thomists

â€¢ ••Luther's own theological background••
	• He was trained in the Occamist school, but later broke with Scholasticism
	• He remained an Occamist in his method and manner of theological argumentation

â€¢ ••Luther's views on philosophy••
	• He criticized the use of Aristotle's philosophy to interpret the Christian gospel
	• He saw Aristotle's metaphysics as "fallacious" and his ethics as "the worst enemy of grace"

â€¢ ••Luther's views on the interpretation of Scripture••
	• He believed that the apostle Paul understood the world differently from philosophers and metaphysicians
	• He emphasized the importance of understanding Scripture in its own terms, rather than through philosophical categories

â€¢ ••Luther's critique of Scholastic theology••
	• He directed his criticism against certain tenets characteristic of the teachings of his own theological masters
	• He was influenced by themes and emphases characteristic of Occamism, which he later broke with

â€¢ ••Luther's views on the role of reason in theology••
	• He emphasized the importance of understanding terms and words in their etymological meaning
	• He believed that reason should be used to interpret Scripture, but not to impose philosophical categories on it

â€¢ ••Luther's views on the relationship between faith and reason••
	• He believed that faith and reason were not mutually exclusive, but should be used in conjunction with each other
	• He emphasized the importance of understanding the spirit behind the writings of the founders of Scholasticism

â€¢ ••Luther's overall approach to theology••
	• He was a "modernist" in his approach to theology, emphasizing the importance of understanding terms and words in their etymological meaning
	• He believed that theology should be grounded in Scripture, rather than in philosophical categories.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Philosophy••
	• Luther believed that philosophy was a vain study that wasted precious time
	• He thought that people should focus on learning Jesus Christ and his teachings

â€¢ ••Rejection of Scholasticism••
	• Luther rejected the Scholastic approach to theology, which he saw as overly focused on philosophy
	• He believed that Scholastics agreed with Aristotle's view that people become righteous through good deeds

â€¢ ••Luther's View of Righteousness••
	• Luther believed that people are not made righteous by doing good deeds, but rather by faith in the gospel
	• He distinguished between the "righteousness of men" (based on human effort) and the "righteousness of God" (based on faith)

â€¢ ••Critique of Aristotle's Ethics••
	• Luther believed that Aristotle's Ethics was opposed to the gospel and was a threat to the Christian faith
	• He saw Aristotle's view of righteousness as a form of Pelagianism, which emphasized human effort over faith

â€¢ ••Luther's View of Human Nature••
	• Luther believed that human beings are inherently unrighteous and in need of salvation
	• He saw human nature as being in a state of sin and unable to achieve righteousness through human effort

â€¢ ••Disagreement with Occamists••
	• Luther disagreed with the Occamists, who emphasized human reason and effort in achieving righteousness
	• He believed that the Occamists, like Gabriel Biel, Pelagianized and emphasized human virtue over faith

â€¢ ••Luther's Focus on Scripture••
	• Luther believed that the Bible should be the primary source of authority in theology
	• He saw philosophy and Scholasticism as secondary to Scripture and often in conflict with it

â€¢ ••Luther's View of Faith••
	• Luther believed that faith was the primary means of achieving righteousness
	• He saw faith as a gift from God, rather than something that could be achieved through human effort

â€¢ ••Luther's Rejection of Pelagianism••
	• Luther rejected the Pelagian view that human beings can achieve righteousness through their own efforts
	• He believed that this view was opposed to the gospel and undermined the need for faith in Christ

â€¢ ••Luther's Emphasis on Salvation by Faith••
	• Luther emphasized the importance of salvation by faith in Christ
	• He saw this as the central message of the gospel and the key to achieving righteousness.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Biel and Scotus••
	• Luther saw Biel and Scotus as Pelagians
	• He believed their views on human nature were incompatible with the Biblical conception of salvation

â€¢ ••Two Key Issues••
	• Teaching that man can love God above all else by natural powers, apart from grace
	• Teaching that under grace, man can perform works of righteousness and earn merits

â€¢ ••Luther's Characterization of the Occamists••
	• He called them "Sautheologen" (theologians who make a mess of interpreting the Christian gospel)
	• He criticized them for being "moralists" and "merit-mongers"

â€¢ ••Distinction between Law and Intention••
	• The Scholastics distinguished between fulfilling God's law according to the substance of the deed (quoad substantiam facti) and according to the intention of God (ad intentionem precipientis)
	• Luther rejected this distinction as a form of Pelagianism

â€¢ ••Biel's View on Love of God••
	• Biel taught that man can love God above all else by his own powers, but not earn eternal life thereby
	• Luther saw this as a form of Pelagianism, where grace is understood as a new requirement of God

â€¢ ••Ockham's View on Good Works••
	• Ockham taught that man can perform good works apart from grace, but no human work can be meritorious
	• Luther rejected this view as a form of Pelagianism, but acknowledged a kinship with Ockham on the importance of divine acceptance

â€¢ ••Luther's View on Human Freedom••
	• Luther emphasized the importance of human freedom, but also divine mercy as the condition of fulfilling the law
	• He rejected the idea that human virtue is the condition of fulfilling the law

â€¢ ••Luther's View on the Gospel••
	• He saw the gospel as teaching that God accepts the sinner by mercifully justifying him
	• He emphasized the importance of forgiveness and divine mercy in the gospel

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Ockham's Teaching••
	• He rejected Ockham's teaching on the divine acceptatio (acceptance) as a form of Pelagianism
	• He directed the 56th thesis of his theses against Ockham's teaching

â€¢ ••Luther's Debt to Biel••
	• He knew large parts of Biel's writings by heart
	• He was aware of his debt to Biel, but rejected Biel's views as a form of Pelagianism

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Disputation Against Scholastic Theology••
	• Luther rejects the doctrine of Ockham that God cannot accept man apart from justifying grace.
	• He argues that this doctrine is contrary to the Bible and leads to a false understanding of salvation.

â€¢ ••Critique of Ockham's Teaching••
	• Luther rejects Ockham's teaching that man can earn merits before God by acting according to his natural moral sense.
	• He argues that this teaching is based on a false understanding of human nature and God's sovereignty.

â€¢ ••Luther's Concerns with Scholastic Theology••
	• Luther is concerned that Scholastic theology does not take seriously man's sin and God's sovereignty.
	• He argues that this theology is based on a false understanding of human nature and God's relationship with humanity.

â€¢ ••Luther's View of Piety••
	• Luther views the piety underlying Scholastic theology as an irreligious affectation of religion.
	• He argues that this piety is based on a selfish love of God for one's own sake, rather than for God's sake.

â€¢ ••The Problem with Covetous Love••
	• Luther argues that loving God for one's own sake is a covetous or selfish love.
	• He believes that this type of love is contrary to the true nature of love, which seeks God's will and glory above all else.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Ordered Love••
	• Luther argues that true love begins with a desire to seek God's will and glory, rather than one's own salvation.
	• He believes that this type of love is the only true and authentic form of love.

â€¢ ••Luther's View of Human Nature••
	• Luther believes that human nature is inherently sinful and unable to do good on its own.
	• He argues that this is why God's sovereignty and freely given mercy and grace are so important.

â€¢ ••The Role of Sacraments••
	• Luther rejects the idea that sacraments can condition human potentiality for good works.
	• He argues that this teaching is based on a false understanding of the role of sacraments in the Christian life.

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of the Actus Eliciti••
	• Luther rejects the idea that humans can elicit acts of loving God from their souls.
	• He argues that this teaching is based on a false understanding of human nature and the role of the Holy Spirit.

â€¢ ••Luther's Focus on God's Sovereignty••
	• Luther emphasizes the importance of God's sovereignty and freely given mercy and grace.
	• He argues that this is the only true and authentic understanding of God's relationship with humanity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Theological Influences••
	• Luther's theology was influenced by mysticism
	• He relied on Dionysius the Areopagite and John Tauler
	• Tauler's ideas on trusting in Jesus alone and not in human works or merits were particularly influential

â€¢ ••Luther's Letter to Staupitz••
	• Luther defended himself against charges of condemning rosaries and good works
	• He stated that he followed the theology of Tauler and the German Theology
	• He emphasized the importance of trusting in Jesus alone for salvation

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Scholasticism••
	• Luther criticized Scholasticism for its authoritarianism and uniformity
	• He believed that Scholastics should allow for debate and dissent
	• He argued that if Scholastics could disagree with each other, they should also allow others to disagree with them

â€¢ ••Luther's Relationship with Scholasticism••
	• Luther did not reject all of Scholasticism, but rather selectively approved or rejected certain ideas
	• He believed in the importance of debate and discussion, even with those he disagreed with

â€¢ ••Luther's Attitude towards Mysticism••
	• Luther was influenced by medieval mystical theologians, but was not a mystic himself
	• He learned from them the importance of humility and resignation

â€¢ ••Luther's Use of Augustine••
	• Luther invoked Augustine against the Scholastics, but did not seem to notice that his criticism could also apply to Augustine

â€¢ ••Luther's Method of Disputation••
	• Luther employed the method of disputation throughout his life
	• This method allowed him to develop his own agreement and disagreement with the positions of others

â€¢ ••Luther's Dialectical Thinking••
	• Luther was a dialectical thinker who said yes as well as no to the teachings of others
	• This approach allowed him to engage with and critique the ideas of others

â€¢ ••Luther's Rejection of Uniformitarianism••
	• Luther rejected the uniformitarianism of the Roman Church and its tendency to impose a single viewpoint on its members
	• He believed in the importance of debate and dissent, even in the face of authority

â€¢ ••Luther's Influence from Various Sources••
	• Luther was influenced by a range of sources, including Dionysius the Areopagite, John Tauler, and the German Theology
	• He selectively approved or rejected ideas from these sources, depending on their compatibility with his own theology.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's influence••
	• Luther was influenced by mystics such as Pseudo-Dionysius, Gerson, and Tauler
	• He felt that Tauler's teachings were particularly important to his own faith

â€¢ ••Tauler's impact••
	• Luther credited Tauler with teaching him more than all the other Scholastics combined
	• He urged his friend Spalatin to read Tauler's sermons, which he had studied with great profit

â€¢ ••The "learning of true piety"••
	• Luther described this as the renunciation of self-righteousness
	• It involves learning to breathe and confide in the righteousness of Christ

â€¢ ••The problem with self-righteousness••
	• Many people try to be righteous and good through their own efforts
	• This leads to a lack of understanding of God's righteousness, which is freely given in Christ

â€¢ ••The solution: faith in Christ••
	• Luther urged his hearers to learn Christ and him crucified
	• They should pray to Christ, despairing of themselves, and recognize that Christ is their righteousness

â€¢ ••The importance of humility••
	• Luther warned against aspiring to purity that makes one feel like a non-sinner
	• Christ dwells only in sinners, and it is for this that he came down from heaven

â€¢ ••The comfort of Christ's love••
	• Luther encouraged his hearers to think about Christ's love and how it comforts and sustains them
	• This love is the key to a quiet conscience and peace with God

â€¢ ••The role of faith••
	• Luther emphasized the importance of faith in despairing of oneself and one's own works
	• This faith is the key to learning that Christ's righteousness is one's own

â€¢ ••Luther's monastic background••
	• As a monk, Luther's thought was shaped by the practice of monastic life
	• He often discussed the mission and trials of the monastic life with his hearers

â€¢ ••The mystic's way of life••
	• Luther's "learning of true piety" describes the mystic's way of life
	• It involves a deep understanding of the practice of religion and the importance of faith in Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Influence of Monastic Life on Luther's Thought••
	• Shaped by the piety of The Psalms understood in terms of Christian doctrine and worship
	• Influenced by the monastic discipline and life

â€¢ ••Observations of Monastic Life••
	• Luther was a sharp observer of his fellow monks and himself
	• Described the whole scale of religious vices and virtues

â€¢ ••Personal Experience and Spiritual Failures••
	• Luther speaks realistically about his own spiritual failures and victories
	• His interpretations of the religious life are touching and moving

â€¢ ••Conflict with the Augustinian Order••
	• The Erfurters were Observants who insisted on a strict enforcement of the rule
	• Luther was sent to Rome as socius itinerarius to lay the views of the Observants before the highest superiors

â€¢ ••Luther's Views on Obedience and Nonconformity••
	• Demands that Christians submit to their superiors (prelati)
	• Condemns individualists (monii) who insist on their own way

â€¢ ••Critique of Puritanical Moral Standards••
	• Compares them to the religion of Baal, a "dreadful righteousness and superstitious religion"
	• Accuses them of being worse than godless people

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Nonconformists••
	• Worship the true God according to their own mind
	• Are conceited individualists who are enemies of God

â€¢ ••Consequences of Nonconformity••
	• Lead to foolish zeal and eccentric religiousness (nimia pietate)
	• Become jealous, profane, and proud despite their claims of humility

â€¢ ••Luther's Mission to Rome••
	• Ended in failure, and Luther urged his fellows to yield to those of higher authority

â€¢ ••Possible Transfer to Wittenberg••
	• May have been transferred from Erfurt to Wittenberg at the behest of Staupitz
	• No solid information about this transfer exists

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Personal Struggle••
	• Luther's inner struggle for a merciful God led to a decision and resolution
	• This understanding of the Christian gospel gave him authority as an interpreter of God's word

â€¢ ••Luther's Monastic Experience••
	• Luther attributed his training and experience as a monk to his becoming a believer in Christ
	• He found himself compelled to criticize and oppose monasticism and the Roman Catholic Church

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Monasticism••
	• He was a faithful and devout monk, but transcended monasticism through his examination of God's requirements
	• He criticized the corruption of the faith in the highest places, including the Curia at Rome

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of Occamist Theology••
	• He examined Occamist theology from a thorough knowledge of it
	• He found it wanting in terms of Biblical-Christian adequacy

â€¢ ••Luther's Relationship with Augustine••
	• He considered Augustine his greatest theological ally
	• However, he found himself driven to go beyond Augustine at a decisive point

â€¢ ••Luther's Religion••
	• His religion was a free trusting in God's gift of forgiveness
	• It entailed an utter self-surrender and reliance on a "foreign righteousness"

â€¢ ••Luther's Rejection of Self-Righteousness••
	• He rejected any form of self-righteousness as a lie and self-deception
	• He searched for truthfulness in the presence of God who can never lie

â€¢ ••Sources of Luther's Religion••
	• The two sources of his religion were Jesus Christ and his conscience
	• His conscience was his knowledge of himself before God at the deepest level of his being

â€¢ ••The Significance of Luther's Lectures••
	• The lectures are a record of Luther's thinking as he prepared for the classroom
	• They demonstrate his sovereignty of faith in Christ alone

â€¢ ••Understanding Luther's Lectures••
	• The reader must never lose sight of the fact that they follow Luther's thinking as a professor of Biblical theology
	• The lectures are a window into Luther's mind and his understanding of the Christian gospel

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Lectures on Romans••
	• Were delivered at the University of Wittenberg
	• Were attended by students, including Augustinian monks
	• Left a vivid impression on his students

â€¢ ••Luther's Teaching Style••
	• Was characterized by a clear and even pace
	• Expounded the text in a logical and concise manner
	• Avoided long-winded explanations and focused on key points

â€¢ ••Student Notes••
	• Several students' notebooks have survived to this day
	• These notebooks show that Luther's lectures were well-organized and easy to follow
	• The notes do not contain any material that is not found in Luther's own text

â€¢ ••Comparison of Luther's Notes and Lectures••
	• Luther's notes were often long-winded and circumstantial
	• His lectures, on the other hand, were clear and succinct
	• He left out many of his own thoughts and criticisms in his lectures

â€¢ ••Purpose of Luther's Lectures••
	• Were intended to help students understand the words and arguments of Paul
	• Were not addressed to any specific audience
	• Were written with the knowledge that only parts of them would be communicated to students

â€¢ ••Luther's Writing Style••
	• Was characterized by a focus on key points and a clear structure
	• Avoided unnecessary digressions and moral philosophy
	• Was influenced by his desire to communicate complex ideas in a simple and concise manner

â€¢ ••Luther's Spirit and Personality••
	• Was described by his students as a "spirit" rather than a "man"
	• Was characterized by a sense of conviction and passion
	• Was able to convey complex ideas in a way that was both clear and compelling

â€¢ ••The Influence of Luther's Lectures••
	• Had a deep impact on his students
	• Helped to shape their understanding of the gospel and its implications
	• Played a significant role in the development of the Protestant Reformation

â€¢ ••The Significance of Luther's Lectures••
	• Provide a unique insight into Luther's thought and teaching style
	• Offer a valuable resource for understanding the historical context of the Reformation
	• Continue to be studied and appreciated by scholars and theologians today

â€¢ ••The Value of Luther's Lectures••
	• Are a testament to Luther's commitment to teaching and education
	• Demonstrate his ability to communicate complex ideas in a clear and concise manner
	• Remain an important part of the theological and historical heritage of the Protestant Reformation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Lectures on Romans••
	• Were not intended to be a published work
	• Did not contain reprimands or admonitions to students
	• Were not delivered as planned

â€¢ ••Significance of the Book••
	• Had no immediate impact on the public
	• Was published nearly 400 years after it was written
	• Became a significant document of Luther's development

â€¢ ••Luther's Theological Curriculum Reform••
	• Introduced Biblical theology in place of philosophical theology
	• Replaced Aristotle with Paul
	• Focused on Biblical exegesis over interpretation of Peter Lombard's Books of Sentences

â€¢ ••Luther's Mission and Ambition••
	• Felt a sense of mission to reform theological studies
	• Did not aspire to be a reformer on a world stage
	• Believed he was simply doing his duty as a theological doctor and teacher

â€¢ ••Luther's Demands for Reform••
	• Demanded reform of theological studies and ecclesiastical practices
	• Advanced these demands in his role as a theological doctor and teacher
	• Did not aspire to be a prophet

â€¢ ••Critique of Scholasticism••
	• Criticized Scholasticism in his interpretation of Paul's letter
	• Called for the abandonment of the philosophical approach to theology

â€¢ ••Attack on Church Practices••
	• Criticized evil practices in the church, including those in high places
	• Called for a basic reform of Christian piety

â€¢ ••Luther's Views on Christian Piety••
	• Believed that Christian piety had become superficial and focused on external displays
	• Criticized the church's focus on material possessions and external practices

â€¢ ••Connection to the Protestant Reformation••
	• Luther's views on Christian piety and the church's practices were a direct precursor to the Protestant Reformation
	• His Theses on the Power of Indulgences sparked an open conflict with the Roman Church

â€¢ ••Legacy of Luther's Lectures on Romans••
	• Became a significant document of Luther's development and a catalyst for the Protestant Reformation
	• Remains an important work in the history of Christian theology and the Protestant Reformation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Luther's Conviction••
	• Luther believed that right teaching would lead to necessary changes in the church.
	• He advocated for a thorough overhaul of the current teaching methods, including canon law, Decretals, Scholastic theology, philosophy, and logic.

â€¢ ••Letter to Jodocus Trutvetter••
	• Luther wrote to his former teacher in 1518, expressing his concerns about the debate on the source and norm of Christian truth.
	• He stated that he feared no one's logic when defending his opinion on the need for reform.

â€¢ ••Commentary on Romans••
	• Luther's commentary on the letter to the Romans is significant because it shows his development towards the Reformation.
	• At the time, Luther was still bound to the Roman Catholic Church and hated heresy and arbitrary defiance of clerical authority.

â€¢ ••Luther's Path to Reformation••
	• Luther became a reformer not because he identified with existing reform movements, but because his understanding of the Christian gospel led him to oppose teachings and practices that contradicted it.
	• He was not influenced by reformers like Wycliffe, Hus, Savonarola, or Erasmus.

â€¢ ••Luther's Understanding of the Gospel••
	• Luther's understanding of the gospel was unique and centered on the Pauline and Augustinian teachings on justification.
	• He achieved clarity on this understanding through his personal struggle for salvation and direct experience of God's wrath and love.

â€¢ ••Personal Character of the Commentary••
	• Luther's commentary on Romans is marked by a pronounced personal character.
	• He speaks personally to his readers, showing how he obtained understanding of Paul's teaching through his personal being.

â€¢ ••Unusual Features of the Commentary••
	• The commentary is unusual because it shows Luther's personal involvement in understanding Paul's teaching.
	• It presents an interpretation that presupposes the personal involvement of the reader.

â€¢ ••Personal Growth of the Commentator••
	• Luther's commentary documents his personal growth as a commentator.
	• At the end of his lectures, Luther speaks of his own personal growth and development.

â€¢ ••Medieval Biblical Exegesis••
	• Luther's commentary was written in the context of medieval Biblical exegesis, Scholastic theology, and the piety and asceticism of the cloister.
	• He navigated the complexities of these influences in his interpretation of the Bible.

â€¢ ••Unique Interpretation••
	• Luther's interpretation of the Bible was unique and creative, quickened by his personal experience of God's wrath and love.
	• He interpreted the whole Bible in terms of the Pauline and Augustinian teachings on justification.

• ••Luther's Commentary on Romans••
	• Written when Luther was still a monk and a faithful member of the Papal Church
	• Explains Luther's understanding of God's promise and man's faith
