	• Faith must be present to ratify God's promise
	• The promise needs faith on the part of the person to whom it is given
• ••Justification of the Sinner••
	• Through the gift of Christ's righteousness
	• Received with humility and the surrender of self
• ••God's Righteousness vs. Human Self-Righteousness••
	• God's righteousness is triumphant over all human self-righteousness
	• Human self-righteousness is destroyed and plucked up

	• This change must take place in our self-awareness and self-esteem

	• Sum and substance: to pull down, pluck up, and destroy all wisdom and righteousness of the flesh
	• To implant, establish, and make large the reality of sin

	• Almost the sole concern of Paul's letter
	• Discussed persistently and with complexity
• ••The Problem of Self-Righteousness••
	• Even the best and sincerest people can be inwardly pleased with themselves
	• This leads to foolishness and a lack of true righteousness

	• We must take pains to destroy and pluck up our own complacent inner feeling
• ••The Importance of Humility••

	• This is the key to true righteousness and salvation

â€¢ ••God's plan for salvation••

	• This righteousness originates in heaven, not on earth

â€¢ ••The role of virtues••

	• They can subject human affections to themselves, making them more powerful
	• The right side of Jordan was more fearful than the left one (Josh. 3:16)

â€¢ ••Christ's desire for us••
	• Christ wants us to be bare of all feeling
	• We should not be afraid of being cast into confusion due to our faults
	• We should not delight in praise and vain joy due to our virtue

â€¢ ••External righteousness••
	• We should not glory in external righteousness that comes from Christ
	• We should not suffer defeat due to sufferings and evils that befall us for Christ's sake

â€¢ ••Christian perfection••

	• Neither honor nor dishonor can affect them
	• They know that whatever honor is done to them is done to Christ

â€¢ ••The importance of humility••

	• We must look for the naked mercy of God to reckon us righteous and wise

â€¢ ••God's judgment••
	• God will judge us, not ourselves
	• We should not anticipate God's action by justifying ourselves (I Cor. 4:3-5)

â€¢ ••Disregarding worldly goods••
	• Some people, like the Jews and heretics, disregard worldly goods for God's sake
	• However, few people regard spiritual goods as nothing for the sake of obtaining Christ's righteousness

â€¢ ••The goal of Christian living••
	• Our goal should be to obtain Christ's righteousness
	• We should regard spiritual goods as nothing in comparison to Christ's righteousness

• ••Luther's Interpretation of Romans••
	• Luther believes that the recipients of the letter to the Romans were not perfect Christians
	• He thinks the apostle Paul wrote to them to mediate their strife and show them their sinfulness
• ••The Importance of Humility••
	• Luther emphasizes the theme of humility in his interpretation of Romans
	• He sees pride as the root of all sin
• ••The Role of the Minister••
	• A prudent minister must hold their ministry in honor and respect
	• They must not exceed their powers or abuse their office in pride
• ••The Dangers of Softness and Austerity••
	• Softness (or lack of authority) leads to contempt and the loss of true leadership
	• Austerity (or tyranny) leads to fear and a desire to destroy rather than edify

	• Softness: leaving the flock to its own devices (Zech. 11:17)
	• Austerity: ruling with force and rigor (Ezek. 34:4)
• ••The Consequences of Softness and Austerity••
	• Both faults lead to a loss of respect and authority
	• They are the root of all the faults of clergymen
• ••The Minister's Duty••
	• To administer their office for the benefit of their subjects
	• To be a "prudent and faithful servant" (Matt. 24:45)
• ••The Importance of Respect••
	• The minister's ministry must be respected by those in their charge
	• They must not permit what belongs to God to be despised
• ••The Minister's Power••
	• The power of their office is given to them to edify, not to destroy
	• They must use their power to make their high position useful to others
• ••The Dangers of Unwise Humility••
	• Practicing familiarity with nearly everybody can lead to a loss of true authority
	• It breeds contempt and is a grave sin

• ••The source of evil••
	• Concupiscence (lust) and irascibility (anger) are the source of all evil
	• These two "beasts" will do more harm when given more power
• ••The apostle's example••
	• The apostle presents himself as an example of opposing these two monsters
	• He magnifies his ministry with high honor to avoid being despised
	• He assures his subjects of his good will to avoid being a tyrant
• ••The clergyman's role••
	• A clergyman should clearly distinguish between himself and his office
	• He should regard himself as the lowliest of all and perform his office with humility
	• He should be willing to give up his office if it's not benefiting his subjects
• ••The sin of a clergyman••
	• A clergyman's sin is depriving his ministry of its fruit through pride or tyranny
	• He will be held strictly responsible for his failure
• ••The meaning of "servant of Jesus Christ"••

	• It shows that the apostle is glad to serve such a Lord
	• It's a great and terrible word that commands respect
• ••The apostle's intention••
	• The apostle doesn't want to call attention to his own personal works
	• He wants to confess that he has received his office from God
	• He wants to emphasize that he's a servant, not a lord or creator
• ••The importance of humility••

	• It's a key characteristic of a true servant of God
• ••The danger of pride••
	• Pride can lead to tyranny and the deprivation of ministry's fruit
	• It's a serious sin that will be held against a clergyman
• ••The importance of serving others••
	• A clergyman's office is given for the welfare of his subjects
	• He should be willing to give up his office if it's not benefiting them
• ••The apostle's self-description••
	• The apostle describes himself as a "servant of Jesus Christ"
	• He wants to emphasize his humility and dependence on God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Service••
	• The author is a servant commissioned to minister to others
	• The ultimate concern of this service is God
	• This service is distinct from the universal service of all people to God

â€¢ ••Types of Service••
	• Universal service: everyone is a servant of God by and for themselves
	• Special service: a servant of God for others and over others, for the sake of others

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Universal Service••
	• Everyone can be certain and glory in it
	• Benefits others and serves them
	• Must be common to all
	• Can be performed without grace
	• Has more worth

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Special Service••
	• Must be done by grace
	• Is more wholesome
	• Is sufficiently known not even to oneself
	• Can be manifested to men in glory

â€¢ ••The Term "Apostle"••
	• Specifies the nature of the author's service and ministry
	• Not all servants and ministers of Jesus Christ are apostles

â€¢ ••Kinds of Men Not Called to Offices of Honor••
	• Pseudo-apostles: false servants sent by the devil
	• Ambitious servants: assume an office for personal gain
	• Forced servants: assume an office without a call from God

â€¢ ••Consequences of Assuming an Office Without a Call••
	• No danger in this world and the next is more to be feared
	• Many are today insensitive to this danger
	• Even those who are called and anointed can fall (e.g. Judas, Saul, David)

â€¢ ••The Dignity of the Term "Apostle"••
	• Heightens the dignity of the author's service and ministry

	• God wills the good, but does not will that all people obey his commandments
	• God's will is not limited by human free will, but rather is the source of human freedom
	• God's will is not the same as human will, and is not bound by the same limitations

â€¢ ••The objection of free will••
	• Some argue that if God wills evil, then human free will is not responsible for sin
	• This objection is not valid, as God's will is not the same as human will
	• Human free will is still responsible for sin, but God's will is not the primary cause

â€¢ ••The mystery of God's will••
	• God's will is a profound and mysterious topic, not easily understood by simple minds
	• The relationship between God's will and human free will is a subtle and complex issue
	• Only experts in theology can fully grasp the nuances of God's will

â€¢ ••God's election and grace••
	• God elects some people to receive his grace, while withholding it from others
	• God's election is not based on human merit, but rather on his own sovereign will
	• God's will is not limited by human free will, but rather is the source of human freedom

â€¢ ••God's purpose in sin••
	• God does not will sin for its own sake, but rather for the sake of his glory and the elect
	• God uses sin to display his power and glory in the elect
	• God's purpose in sin is not to condemn, but rather to redeem and glorify

â€¢ ••Scriptural examples••
	• God incited and hardened Pharaoh to show his power (Ex. 4:21-23)
	• God says "I will have mercy on whom I have mercy" (Rom. 9:15)
	• These examples illustrate God's purpose in sin, which is to display his glory and power

â€¢ ••Faith and understanding••
	• Faith is the conviction of things unseen, and is necessary to understand God's will
	• The relationship between God's will and human free will is a mystery that cannot be fully understood
	• We must trust in God's goodness and sovereignty, even when we do not fully understand his will.

â€¢ ••God's sovereignty and mercy••
	• God causes the Gentiles to fall in order to show his mercy to them
	• He permits evil to occur, but not against his will

â€¢ ••The problem of human inability••

â€¢ ••The reason for the penalty••

â€¢ ••The relationship between soul and body••

	• They receive shame instead of glory for their sin

â€¢ ••The importance of glorifying God••
	• Humans must glorify God inwardly and outwardly
	• This is the opposite of idolatry, which involves transferring God's glory to something else

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Idolatry and Shame••
	• Those who turn away from God's glory should be dishonored in their hearts and bodies.

â€¢ ••Not All Idolaters Are Guilty••
	• Not all idolaters have committed terrible acts, but many have.

â€¢ ••Three Ways of Being Given Up••
	• The apostle lists three ways of being given up to sin: uncleanness, effeminacy, and lust.
	• These actions can be committed by different individuals or by the same person.

â€¢ ••All Sinners in Need of Grace••
	• Even if individuals did not commit all these crimes, they were still sinners in need of God's grace.

â€¢ ••Judging Others••
	• The apostle criticizes those who set themselves up as judges over others despite their own sinful actions.
	• They had committed crimes similar to those they judged others for.

â€¢ ••Uncleanness and Effeminacy••
	• Uncleanness and effeminacy are forms of dishonor and shame.
	• These actions include fornication, adultery, and unnatural abuse of the body.

â€¢ ••Definition of Uncleanness••
	• Uncleanness refers to intentional and individual pollution of the body.
	• This can be caused by lascivious thoughts, physical contact, or obscene movements.

â€¢ ••Involuntary Pollution••
	• Pollution that occurs involuntarily, such as during sleep, is not considered uncleanness.
	• This type of pollution is not intentional and is not the same as uncleanness.

â€¢ ••Chastity and Honor••
	• The body is ordained for honorable marriage and chastity.
	• Uncleanness and effeminacy are forms of pollution that dishonor the body.

â€¢ ••Victory Over the Body••
	• A young person can achieve victory over the body's desires by cultivating devotion to God.
	• This is a better victory than trying to suppress the body's desires through other means.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Righteousness and Unrighteousness••
	• Righteousness is believing and obeying God's commandments
	• Unrighteousness is disobedience and sin
	• Sin is disobedience of God's commandments (Ambrose)

â€¢ ••Iniquity••
	• Iniquity is the sin of self-righteousness, chosen out of a foolish zeal for piety
	• It involves doing what seems right to oneself, rather than what one is bound to do
	• Iniquity is relative, comparing to true righteousness and self-righteousness

â€¢ ••Malice••
	• Malice is the tendency to do evil despite receiving good from God or others
	• It involves using good for evil purposes
	• Malice is the opposite of goodness

â€¢ ••Goodness••
	• Goodness is the tendency to do good, even when hindered or checked by evil
	• It involves making evil serve for good
	• Goodness is not just doing good when prospering, but also when opposed

â€¢ ••Benignity and Malignity••
	• Benignity is friendly readiness to live with others, kindness, and indulgence
	• It is of two kinds: perfect (Christian) and human (worldly)
	• Malignity is the perverse tendency to do ill to others and be vindictive

â€¢ ••Types of Malignity••
	• Brutal unkindness: harming the grateful and good, not just the evil
	• Human and imperfect malignity: seeking to harm others, but making a halt before benefactors

â€¢ ••Fruit of the Spirit••
	• The fruit of the spirit is goodness and kindness (Gal. 5:22)
	• This involves doing good and being kind, even when opposed

â€¢ ••Wickedness••
	• Wickedness is mental perverseness that causes one to avoid helping others
	• It involves intentionally avoiding opportunities to protect others from evil

â€¢ ••Augustine's Interpretation••
	• Blessed Augustine interprets wickedness as avoiding opportunities to help others (On Order)

â€¢ ••Contrasting Virtues••
	• Virtues like goodness, benignity, and kindness are contrasted with vices like malice, malignity, and wickedness
	• These virtues and vices are defined in relation to each other and to God's commandments.

â€¢ ••Glory and Honor••

â€¢ ••The Purpose of God's Wrath••

â€¢ ••The Gentiles' Perdition••
	• The Gentiles will perish without the law of the written tradition.

	• "Without the law" can mean without the co-operation of the law.

â€¢ ••The Gentiles' Obligation••
	• They were under no obligation to the Mosaic law.
	• They did not receive the rites and ceremonies of the law.

â€¢ ••The Spiritual Law••
	• The Gentiles received a spiritual law, impressed upon all.
	• This law is the natural law, known by everyone.

â€¢ ••The Natural Law••
	• The natural law is the basis for the moral interpretation of the Scriptures.
	• It is the law that everyone knows and is bound to obey.

â€¢ ••The Cause of Perdition••
	• The cause of the Gentiles' perdition is not their failure to observe the Mosaic law.
	• It is their failure to observe the natural law.

â€¢ ••The Jews' Situation••
	• The Jews received the law and kept it not.
	• They will be judged for their failure to observe the law.

â€¢ ••The Gentiles' Situation••
	• The Gentiles will perish without the law that was handed down to them.
	• They will perish by a law that is the same, but not handed down in writing.

â€¢ ••The Question of Salvation••
	• Can Gentiles who fulfill the law according to their conscience be saved?
	• The apostle seems to assert that some have done or do the things of the law by their natural ability.

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ••
	• Salvation is by Christ alone.
	• However, those who fulfill the law may obtain grace and be in Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Mercy and Prevenience••
	• God's mercy is a special prevenience that allows all people to receive the light and grace they need for salvation.
	• This is evident in the lives of individuals like Job, Naaman, and Jethro, who received God's mercy despite not following the law.

â€¢ ••Fulfilling the Law••
	• The apostle says that uncircumcision (Gentiles) can fulfill the law if they keep its ordinances.
	• This means that Gentiles have fulfilled the law, but lacked something that God supplied through Christ.

â€¢ ••God's Forbearance••
	• God's forbearance is a valid excuse for the lack of knowledge or understanding that leads to sin.
	• God supplies what is lacking in order to make it perfect through Christ.

â€¢ ••No Respect of Persons••
	• God shows no respect of persons, meaning he treats everyone equally.
	• This is evident in the fact that God's mercy is available to all, regardless of their background or circumstances.

â€¢ ••Critique of Jewish Pride••
	• The Jews boasted of receiving the law and being its bearers and disciples.
	• They resented the idea that Gentiles were put on the same level with them in terms of good and evil.

â€¢ ••God's Equality••
	• God treats everyone equally, regardless of their background or circumstances.
	• This is evident in the fact that God says "to the Jew first and also to the Greek" (Rom. 2:9-10).

â€¢ ••Heretics and Spiritual Pride••
	• Heretics and spiritually proud individuals presume that they have elected themselves before others.
	• They do not know that God elects and favors only those who despise themselves and prefer others to themselves.

â€¢ ••Piercing the Pride of Gentiles••
	• The Gentiles puff themselves up with the excuse that they did not know the law.
	• God replies that they will perish without the law, just as they observe their own law.

â€¢ ••The Law of Humility••
	• The law of humility is to make an offering to God of our own will, judgment, and glory.
	• We must also make the same offering to our fellow men, whom we generally try to outstrip.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Humility••
	• The entire Scripture teaches nothing else than humility.
	• We must be subject not only to God but also to every creature, and want all and everything to be subject to us is a perverse tendency of our mind.

â€¢ ••Justification through Obedience••
	• Those who do the law will be justified (Rom. 2:13)
	• This can be understood in two ways, as explained by Augustine (On the Spirit and the Letter, 26)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Justification and Righteousness••
	• Justification means being considered and declared righteous by God
	• This is the sense of the context in Romans 2:13
	• The doers of the law will be justified and regarded as righteous

â€¢ ••The Law and Righteousness••
	• The law is not enough to make someone righteous before God
	• No one is righteous before God except through faith in Christ
	• The law is not a means of justification, but rather a means of showing one's faith

â€¢ ••The Gentiles and the Law••
	• The Gentiles who have not the law can still do the things of the law
	• This is not because they are inherently righteous, but rather because they are doing good works

â€¢ ••Augustine's Interpretation••
	• Augustine understands the passage in a double way
	• First, he sees the Gentiles as believers who were justified by the grace of Christ
	• Second, he sees them as people who do some good, but are not truly righteous

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Gentiles••
	• Augustine interprets "by nature" as referring to the corrupted nature of humanity
	• However, he also sees it as possible to understand "by nature" as referring to the natural ability of humans to do good

â€¢ ••The Role of Grace••
	• Grace is not negated by human nature, but rather restores it
	• Grace is not given in recognition of human merit, but rather as a gift

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Good Works and Salvation••
	• Good works do not secure eternal salvation for a godless person
	• However, good works can be a sign of a person's faith and preparation for salvation

â€¢ ••The Doers of the Law••
	• The doers of the law are righteous, but not in the sense of being inherently righteous
	• Rather, they are righteous because they have faith in Christ and are doing good works

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Passage••
	• The passage is contrasting the Gentiles who have faith in Christ with the Jews who presumptuously rely on the law
	• The passage is also highlighting the importance of faith and good works in achieving righteousness

â€¢ ••The Implications of the Passage••
	• The passage implies that faith in Christ is necessary for salvation
	• The passage also implies that good works are a necessary part of the Christian life, but not sufficient for salvation on their own.

â€¢ ••The law written in their hearts vs. the work of the law••
	• The law written in their hearts refers to the law of Christ and the fulfillment of the law of Moses.
	• It is a law without law, without measure, without end, and without limit.

â€¢ ••How the Gentiles show that the work of the law is written in their hearts••
	• They do the things of the law.
	• Their conscience gives them testimony about themselves, both good and bad.

â€¢ ••The conscience as a witness••
	• It gives good testimony about good deeds.
	• It gives bad testimony about evil deeds, showing that they have done evil.

â€¢ ••The significance of remorse••
	• Remorse shows that they have recognized the evil they have done.
	• It proves that they have a knowledge of good and evil.

â€¢ ••The relationship between the law and the conscience••
	• The law was not unknown to them, but they had a knowledge of good and evil.
	• Their conscience is a witness to their actions, both good and bad.

â€¢ ••The implications of this interpretation••
	• All people, both Jews and Gentiles, are sinners in need of the grace of God.
	• The law is not a means of salvation, but rather a demonstration of sin.

• ••God's promise to the Jews••
	• God's promise to the Jews is not nullified because most of them did not believe.
	• Those who did not believe are liars, not God, who has kept his promise in all children of the promise.
• ••Justification by God's truthfulness••
	• The phrase "That thou mayest be justified" (Rom. 3:4) must be understood in the context of God's truthfulness.
	• God is justified by the confession of our sin, not by our unrighteousness.
• ••God's righteousness••
	• Some people think God's righteousness is proved by punishing our unrighteousness.
	• However, this is not the topic the apostle is discussing in this context.
• ••The psalm's meaning••
	• The psalm (Ps. 51:4) says that God is justified by the confession of our sin.
	• Our sin does not justify God, but rather our confession of sin humbles us and glorifies God.
• ••The role of confession••
	• The acknowledgment and confession of our unrighteousness give God glory and praise.
	• This shows how needful to salvation God's righteousness is.
• ••The relationship between God's righteousness and our unrighteousness••
	• Our unrighteousness does not set off God's righteousness.
	• Only carnal men can find a meaning in the psalm that suggests our unrighteousness commends God's righteousness.
• ••God's righteousness in himself••
	• The apostle is not speaking of God's righteousness in himself, but rather of his righteousness in relation to us.
	• God's righteousness makes us righteous, and he alone is righteous with respect to us.
• ••The purpose of God's punishment••
	• God's punishment of our unrighteousness is not to prove his righteousness, but rather to show his hatred for our sin.
• ••The role of opposites••
	• Some people think that our unrighteousness incidentally commends God's righteousness, like opposites in a picture.
	• However, the apostle denies that our unrighteousness can in any way set off God's righteousness.
• ••The importance of understanding the context••
	• The apostle's argument is based on the meaning of the psalm in its own context, not just in the context of Romans 3.
	• The apostle draws conclusions from the psalm that are relevant to the topic at hand.

	• God's righteousness is more admirable when compared to our wickedness
	• This is not the mode of righteousness the apostle is speaking of
• ••Third Way: God's Working in Us••
	• God justifies us by faith, making us righteous
	• This is the righteousness of faith and the righteousness God works in us
• ••Corollary: Our Unrighteousness Does Not Commend God••
	• Our sin does not justify or commend God
	• Instead, our acknowledgment and confession of sin commend God's righteousness
• ••Acknowledgment of Sin Commends God••
	• When we acknowledge our sin, we yearn for God's righteousness
	• This confession makes us commend God's righteousness
• ••God's Righteousness is Recommended••
	• God is righteous by making us righteous
	• This is the true way of benevolence and humaneness
• ••Comparison to a Craftsman••
	• A craftsman can recommend himself in three ways
	• God's righteousness is similar to the third way: transmitting mastery to others
• ••God's Righteousness is Worthy of Praise••
	• God is worthy of praise because he makes us similar to himself
	• This is the true way of benevolence and humaneness
• ••God's Righteousness is Not Conceived in Pride••
	• God's righteousness is not conceived in pride or conceit
	• Instead, it is a demonstration of benevolence and humaneness

â€¢ ••The Problem of Unbelief••

	• The apostle says that unbelief does not make God's truth without effect.

â€¢ ••The Objection to the Apostle's Statement••
	• The apostle anticipates an objection to his statement that the promises are committed to the circumcision.
	• The objection is that no one who is a Jew outwardly has received the promise.

â€¢ ••The Answer to the Objection••
	• The apostle says that the promises were given to the Jews according to the flesh, but not fulfilled.
	• This does not mean that God did not keep his promise, but rather that the Jews did not receive it in faith.

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Apostle's Argument••
	• The apostle is arguing that God's truth and faithfulness are not made ineffective by human unbelief.
	• He is also highlighting the unique position of the Jews and the profit of circumcision.

â€¢ ••The risk of changing works of grace into works of the law••
	• When we become satisfied with our good works, we can stop growing and become proud.
	• This can turn works of grace into works of the law, and righteousness of God into righteousness of man.

â€¢ ••The importance of ongoing justification••
	• All righteous works done in grace are a preparatory stage in the progress of justification.
	• We should always ask and wait to be justified, and not regard ourselves as righteous.

â€¢ ••The humility of saints••
	• Saints always acknowledge and confess themselves as sinners.
	• They seek to be justified and detest their sins.

â€¢ ••God's wonderfulness in his saints••
	• God regards as righteous those who acknowledge and bewail themselves as sinners.
	• He condemns those who think they are righteous.

â€¢ ••The importance of confession and prayer••
	• Righteous men pray for their sins and confess them to the Lord.
	• This is a sign of humility and a desire for ongoing justification.

â€¢ ••The contrast between the righteous and the ungodly••
	• The righteous recognize themselves as sinners and hate their sin.
	• The ungodly recognize their righteousness and are pleased with themselves about it.

â€¢ ••The need for ongoing growth and progress••
	• We should go from strength to strength and from glory to glory.
	• This is in accordance with the saying "Let him who is righteous be justified still" (Rev. 22:11).

â€¢ ••The role of Christ as King of the Jews••
	• Christ is the King of the Jews, i.e., of those who confess that they are always beset by sin.
	• He is the ruler of believers and confessors.

â€¢ ••The importance of recognizing our sinfulness••
	• We should always recognize our sinfulness and acknowledge our need for justification.
	• This is a sign of humility and a desire for ongoing growth and progress.

â€¢ ••The meaning of "glory in God"••

â€¢ ••The phrase "glory of God"••

	• Example: "But thou, O Lord, art the lifter up of my head and my glory" (Ps. 3:3)

â€¢ ••The Master of the Sentences' interpretation••
	• Interprets "to the showing of his righteousness" as forgiveness of past sins
	• Sees it as God's righteousness in forgiving the sins of the past

â€¢ ••A better interpretation••
	• Reads "to the showing of his righteousness" as forgiving past sins to declare God's righteousness
	• Sees it as God's power to justify and forgive sins

â€¢ ••The purpose of forgiveness••
	• Not to enable sinning, but to show God's righteousness
	• To prove God's power to justify and forgive sins

â€¢ ••The limitation of forgiveness••
	• God does not forgive all sins, lest people sin freely
	• Example: "If, then, all sins are taken away by Christ, let us do what we want" (Gal. 5:13)

â€¢ ••The nature of God's righteousness••
	• Refers to God's power to justify and make righteous
	• Example: "that thou mayest be justified in thy words" (Rom. 3:4)

â€¢ ••The phrase "of former sins"••
	• Indicates that God does not forgive all sins
	• Prevents people from sinning freely, thinking they are forgiven

â€¢ ••The apostle's purpose••
	• To show that God's righteousness is His power to justify and forgive sins
	• To contrast with a foolish and carnal interpretation of His words

â€¢ ••Understanding the Law••
	• The law is established in two ways: intrinsically and extrinsically
	• Intrinsically, the law is formally established when its words and prescriptions are invoked
	• Extrinsically, the law is established when its prescriptions are complied with

â€¢ ••The Law's Purpose••
	• The law is meant to be obeyed and its prescriptions followed
	• When the law is obeyed, its words "prevail" and its purpose is fulfilled

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Disobedience••
	• When the law is disobeyed, it is "made of no effect" and its prescriptions are not followed
	• This leads to the law being "destroyed" and its purpose being unfulfilled

â€¢ ••The Role of Faith••
	• Faith is necessary to fulfill the law and establish it in oneself
	• Without faith, the law is not fulfilled and its purpose is not achieved

â€¢ ••The Law of Works vs. the Law of Faith••
	• The law of works puffs up and induces glorying, as it focuses on outward obedience
	• The law of faith, on the other hand, focuses on giving what is commanded, rather than just doing it

â€¢ ••The Problem with the Law of Works••
	• The law of works leads to pride and a lack of humility, as people believe they have already attained righteousness
	• This leads to a lack of seeking justification and a fervent longing for righteousness

â€¢ ••The Importance of Humility••
	• Humility is necessary to recognize one's sinfulness and seek justification
	• Without humility, the law of works leads to pride and a lack of desire for righteousness

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Faith and the Law••
	• Faith is necessary to fulfill the law and establish it in oneself
	• Without faith, the law is not fulfilled and its purpose is not achieved

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ••
	• Christ's death and resurrection provide the means for justification and righteousness
	• Through faith in Christ, the law is fulfilled and its purpose is achieved

â€¢ ••The Boasting of the Law••
	• The law of works leads to boasting and pride, as people believe they have already attained righteousness
	• The law of faith, on the other hand, excludes boasting and pride, as it focuses on giving what is commanded.

	• We receive wisdom, righteousness, sanctification, and redemption through faith in Christ
• ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is a fundamental part of human nature
	• The will resists the good and inclines toward evil, making it impossible to fulfill the law without grace

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Problem of Security••
	• Many people rely on a false sense of security, thinking that wanting to be righteous is enough.
	• This security leads to indolence and a lack of effort to achieve righteousness.

â€¢ ••The True Meaning of "Wanting"••
	• The apostle Paul's "to will" (Rom. 7:18) means to demonstrate a desire for righteousness with all one's powers and efforts.
	• This desire is not yet fulfilled in this life, but only in the life to come.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Human Will••
	• Human will is not just a small, elicited act, but a deep and ongoing desire for righteousness.
	• This desire is not yet perfect and is often hindered by internal sin.

â€¢ ••The Cause of Hypocrisy••
	• The sense of security is the mother of hypocrites and causes hypocrisy.
	• When people feel secure, they give up the fear of God and become proud.

â€¢ ••The Role of Fear in the Christian Life••
	• Fear of God is necessary for humility and a desire to run to his grace.
	• Without fear, people fall into sin and do not love God above everything.

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Security••
	• When people feel secure, they do not confess their sin and do not seek to drive out internal sin.
	• This leads to a lack of repentance and a failure to hate sin.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Imploring Mercy••
	• Imploring God's mercy with fear and trembling is necessary to avoid having sin imputed to us.
	• This is a merciful counsel of God that forces us to tire of this life and long for his mercy.

â€¢ ••The Divine Decree••
	• God has decided not to impute sin to anyone who implores his mercy with fear and trembling.
	• This decree is a merciful counsel that forces us to seek God's mercy.

â€¢ ••The Reproach of Hypocrites••
	• Hypocrites and people who regard themselves as holy are reproached for their sense of security.
	• This sense of security is considered the source of pride and a lack of fear of God.

â€¢ ••The Need to Drive Out Internal Sin••
	• People must seek to drive out internal sin, not just consider the actual sin of deed, word, or thought.
	• This requires a deep and ongoing desire for righteousness and a fear of God.

	• "Pesha'" (crimes, actual sins, crooked actions)

â€¢ ••Unrighteousness••
	• Imputed to us on account of our crimes or sin

â€¢ ••The Danger of Pride••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Psalm 32••
	• Luther interprets the psalm in his lectures on Romans
	• He sees it as a passage about God's righteousness and the non-imputation of sin

â€¢ ••Understanding "sin" and "unrighteousness"••
	• Luther argues that "sin" should be understood as "unrighteousness" to be consistent with the apostle's intention
	• This is because the apostle wants to prove that righteousness is given without works by the reckoning of God

â€¢ ••The concept of non-imputation••
	• Luther explains that God will not impute unrighteousness to anyone, regardless of good works, unless their sin has been covered
	• This covering of sin happens through penitence, baptism, and a prayerful fear of God

â€¢ ••Confusion in the translation••
	• Luther notes that the translation of the psalm is confusing, with words like "sin" and "unrighteousness" being used interchangeably
	• He argues that the Hebrew text consistently maintains the distinction between "sin" and "unrighteousness"

â€¢ ••Correct translation of key phrases••
	• Luther provides correct translations of key phrases, such as "Blessed are they whose crimes are forgiven and whose sin is covered"
	• He also notes that the translator incorrectly uses the plural "iniquities" and the singular "unrighteousness" in certain places

â€¢ ••The role of confession and forgiveness••
	• Luther explains that confession and forgiveness are key to covering sin and avoiding the imputation of unrighteousness
	• He notes that everyone will confess their unrighteousness before God, and that God will forgive them and not impute unrighteousness

â€¢ ••The nature of sin and unrighteousness••
	• Luther describes sin as a "radical and deep" sin that is the root of all unrighteousness
	• He notes that this sin is covered through penitence, baptism, and a prayerful fear of God

â€¢ ••The consequences of unrighteousness••
	• Luther explains that unrighteousness leads to many scourges and punishments
	• He notes that the ungodly person who justifies themselves and despises God's righteousness is guilty of a double sin

â€¢ ••The importance of understanding the Hebrew text••
	• Luther emphasizes the importance of understanding the Hebrew text to avoid confusion and misinterpretation
	• He notes that the Hebrew text consistently maintains the distinction between "sin" and "unrighteousness"

â€¢ ••The relevance of Psalm 51••
	• Luther notes that Psalm 51 also uses the terms "iniquity" and "sin" interchangeably, leading to confusion
	• He provides correct translations of key phrases in the psalm, such as "blot out my iniquity" and "wash me thoroughly from my iniquity"

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Original Sin••
	• The Hebrew text of Psalm 51:5 speaks of original sin
	• The verse "Behold, I was conceived in iniquities" refers to the sin of Adam being passed down to the child

â€¢ ••Conception and Sin••
	• The child is conceived with the "tinder" of concupiscence (sin)
	• The mother does not sin by giving birth, but the child is born a sinner

â€¢ ••Personal Responsibility••
	• The child does not confess the sin of someone else, but their own
	• The child's sin is their own, but also not their own (i.e., inherited)

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is not created by the child, but is present from the beginning
	• Sin is a common inheritance of all humanity

â€¢ ••The Role of the Law••
	• The law (e.g. "You shall not covet") reveals the child's sin
	• The child's sin is their own, and they have the ability to choose to sin or not

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Sin and Righteousness••
	• Righteousness is not a quality of the soul, but rather a reckoning of God
	• A person is considered righteous by God when they confess their unrighteousness and implore divine righteousness

â€¢ ••The Human Condition••
	• All people are born in iniquity (unrighteousness)
	• All people die in iniquity, but are considered righteous through faith in God's word

â€¢ ••Scriptural Evidence••
	• Moses in Genesis 8:21 and Exodus 34:7 assert that all men are sinners
	• The imagination and thought of man's heart are prone to evil from youth

â€¢ ••The Role of Faith••
	• Faith in God's word is necessary for righteousness
	• God mercifully regards people as righteous when they confess their unrighteousness and implore divine righteousness

â€¢ ••The Nature of Righteousness••
	• Righteousness is not a quality of the soul, but rather a gift from God
	• Righteousness is a reckoning of God, not a inherent quality of the person.

â€¢ ••The Role of the Law••
	• The law works wrath, not salvation.

	• The promise of salvation cannot be fulfilled through the law, which works wrath.

â€¢ ••Objection to Abraham's fatherhood••
	• The objection is that Abraham's fatherhood is not unique, as other leaders are also called "fathers" by their people.
	• This fatherhood is temporal and vicarious, and can be held by multiple people.

â€¢ ••Response to the objection••
	• The promise of God to Abraham is not limited to his physical descendants.
	• The inheritance promised to Abraham is now actualized in Christ, who is the King and Lord of the nations.

â€¢ ••Christ's victory over the nations••
	• Christ has established his rule over the nations through the "sword of the word" (Hebrews 4:12).
	• He makes the ungodly godly and establishes his rule over them, even in their own land and possessions.

â€¢ ••God's power to raise up children••
	• God has the power to raise up children from stones, as John the Baptist says in Luke 3:8.
	• This confirms the promise of God to Abraham that he can become the father of many nations, even if not by his own strength.

â€¢ ••Abraham's faith••
	• Abraham believed in hope against hope, trusting that he would become the father of many nations.
	• He was not weak in faith, but was strengthened in faith, giving glory to God.

â€¢ ••Definition of hope••
	• Hope can be understood in two ways: as something naturally hoped for, or as something supernaturally hoped for.
	• In both cases, hope is what one hopes for, but not the power of hoping.

â€¢ ••Difference between human and Christian hope••
	• Human hope is not against hope, but in accordance with what is hoped for.
	• Human hope is negative, relying on what has begun to happen, and anticipating the realization of what one hopes for.

â€¢ ••Characteristics of human hope••
	• Human hope wants to be certain and informed.
	• Human hope is directed to what can readily become possible.

â€¢ ••Abraham's hope••
	• Abraham's hope was not based on what he could see or anticipate.
	• His hope was based on the promise of God, which he trusted would be fulfilled.

â€¢ ••Importance of faith••
	• Faith is the power that enables us to trust in God's promises, even when they seem impossible.
	• Faith gives glory to God, and strengthens us in our trust in Him.

â€¢ ••Tribulation's Effect••
	• Tribulation can either make us more carnal and weak or more spiritual and strong
	• It reveals our true character and nature

â€¢ ••Impatience••
	• Tribulation does not make us impatient, but reveals the impatience that was already in us
	• We can learn what kind of person we are in times of tribulation

â€¢ ••Reverence for the Cross••
	• Those who outwardly reverence the cross but detest tribulations are hypocrites
	• Tribulations are the cross of Christ, and we must take it up and follow Him

â€¢ ••Enemies of the Cross••
	• The enemies of the cross of Christ are those who do not take up their cross and follow Him
	• This includes theologians and leaders who only think of external enemies, such as the Turks and Jews

â€¢ ••God's Severity and Kindness••
	• God's severity can bring tribulation that is not good
	• God's kindness can bring tribulation that works good in us

â€¢ ••Weakness and Recognition••
	• Our weakness can prevent us from recognizing the nature, power, and purpose of tribulation
	• We should adore tribulation as if it were the cross of Christ itself

â€¢ ••Corollary••
	• Tribulation can have a twofold effect: it can either make us more carnal or more spiritual
	• We should learn to recognize and respond to tribulation in a way that reveals our true character and nature.

â€¢ ••Interpretation of "according to the time"••
	• Some relate it to the sentence that follows, meaning Jesus died in time despite being immortal in eternity
	• Others read it as "he died according to the time when we were weak", referring to the time when humans were not yet righteous
	• Others relate it to the preceding sentence, meaning humans were weak according to time but already righteous in God's predestination

â€¢ ••Original sin vs. actual sin••
	• The apostle speaks of original sin, not actual sin
	• Original sin entered the world through one man, Adam
	• Actual sin comes into the world through many individuals

â€¢ ••Proof of original sin••
	• The phrase "by one man" suggests a single origin of sin
	• Blessed Augustine argues that if the apostle meant to refer to sin through imitation, he would have mentioned the devil as the originator
	• Chrysostom agrees that the sin referred to is the one that comes from Adam's disobedience

â€¢ ••Meaning of "enters into the world"••
	• The term "world" refers to human beings, not heaven and earth
	• Sin does not enter into others, but remains in each individual
	• The physical world is insensitive and not capable of sin

â€¢ ••Consequences of original sin••
	• Sin and death passed upon all men through one man, Adam
	• The world becomes guilty and sinful through one man
	• Many became sinners through the trespass of one

â€¢ ••Original sin is not a matter of imitation••
	• The apostle refers to the sin that entered the world through propagation, not imitation
	• Adam's sin is the origin of original sin, not the devil's

â€¢ ••Original sin is a matter of propagation••
	• The apostle speaks of the sin that passed from one to all by propagation
	• Adam's disobedience is the origin of original sin
	• The human race had its beginning through Adam's propagation

â€¢ ••Actual sin is different from original sin••
	• Actual sin comes into the world through many individuals
	• Each individual brings their own sin with them
	• Actual sin is not the same as original sin

â€¢ ••The world is guilty and sinful••
	• The world becomes guilty and sinful through one man, Adam
	• The world is not capable of sin, but human beings are
	• The world is not the same as heaven and earth

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The apostle speaks of original sin, not actual sin
	• Original sin entered the world through one man, Adam
	• The consequences of original sin are far-reaching and affect all humanity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Death is not the result of personal sin••
	• People who have not sinned die too
	• Death is death through sin, and would not be death without sin

â€¢ ••Sin is universal, not personal••
	• If sin were personal, it would be false to say death entered the world through sin
	• The apostle would have said death came through the will of God instead

â€¢ ••Death passes upon all men••
	• Even if death enters through personal sin, it befalls only the person who committed it
	• The law says "fathers shall not be put to death for the children" (Deut. 24:16)

â€¢ ••The apostle speaks of one sin, not many••
	• He uses the singular "sin" instead of the plural "sins"
	• He compares this one sin to many others in Rom. 5:16

â€¢ ••All have sinned in their own sin••
	• There is not another kind of personal sin "in which all have sinned"
	• Everyone sins in their own sin, not in the sin of Adam

â€¢ ••Original sin was unknown until Moses••
	• Actual sin was in the world before Moses, but original sin was not revealed until then
	• Moses revealed original sin in Gen. 3:16 ff

â€¢ ••Adam's sin is not an imitation of actual sin••
	• Pelagius wanted to understand Adam's sin as an imitation of actual sin
	• But the apostle says Adam's sin is a figure of Christ, not an imitation of actual sin

â€¢ ••Adam is a figure of Christ, not all men••
	• Adam's sin is a figure of Christ's sin, not a figure of all men's sin
	• If Adam's sin were an actual sin, all men would be a figure of Christ

â€¢ ••The apostle emphasizes the singular "sin"••
	• He uses the singular "sin" to emphasize that there is only one original sin
	• He denies that many sinned except one, and that only one man sinned

â€¢ ••Original sin is the privation of original righteousness••
	• According to Scholastic theologians, original sin is the privation or lack of original righteousness
	• This is based on Aristotle's category of quality, where the opposite of righteousness is its lack

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Original Sin and the Transgression of Adam••
	• Augustine interprets Romans 5:19 as referring to the transgression of Adam as the original sin.
	• This interpretation is based on the phrase "they have sinned" being related to an actual deed, not just a condition of guilt.

â€¢ ••Two Interpretations of Romans 5:19••
	• The first interpretation: "All have sinned in the sin of one" (Adam).
	• The second interpretation: "While one sinned, all sinned" (i.e., Adam's sin is the cause of all subsequent sin).

â€¢ ••Isaiah 43:26-27 and the Concept of Original Sin••
	• The passage suggests that one's sinfulness is inherited from Adam, the first sinner.
	• This idea is used to justify the concept of original sin.

â€¢ ••Luther's Interpretation of Romans 5:19••
	• Luther follows Faber's interpretation of "in quo" (in that) as neuter, while Lyra interprets it as masculine.

â€¢ ••Augustine's Explanation of Romans 5:13••
	• Augustine explains that sin was present in the world before the law, but it was not imputed (i.e., not counted as sin) until the law came.

â€¢ ••Augustine's View on the Law and Sin••
	• The law did not take away sin, but rather made it manifest.
	• Sin was not imputed when there was no law, but it began to be counted (i.e., acknowledged) when the law was given.

â€¢ ••Augustine's Forced Interpretation of "Until the Law"••
	• Augustine connects the phrase "until the law" with the statement "There was sin."
	• However, this interpretation is forced and not necessary if one understands the phrase in connection with the negation "It was not imputed."

â€¢ ••Alternative Interpretation of Romans 5:13••
	• The phrase "until the law" can be understood as meaning that sin was not imputed or known until the law came.
	• The law brought sin to the attention of the mind, making it known.

â€¢ ••Augustine's View on the Relationship between Sin and the Law••
	• Sin existed in the world before the law, but it was not acknowledged until the law came.
	• The law did not call sin into being, but rather made it known.

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Romans 5:13••
	• The passage means that sin was present in the world always, but it was not acknowledged or imputed until the law came.
	• The law brought sin to the attention of the mind, making it known.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Understanding the Law••
	• The law was not given to promote sin, but rather sin abounded when the law entered (Rom. 7:13)
	• God's intention was not to increase sin, but to show humanity its sinful nature

â€¢ ••Living in Grace••
	• We should not continue in sin to experience more of God's grace (Rom. 6:1-2)
	• Instead, we should live in a way that is pleasing to God, even though we are still tempted by sin

â€¢ ••Double Servitude••
	• We are under the law of God in our minds, but under the law of sin in our flesh (Rom. 7:23)
	• This double servitude is a result of our sinful nature, but it does not mean we are helpless

â€¢ ••Resisting Sin••
	• We should not yield to evil desires and sin, even though they continue to tempt us (Rom. 6:12)
	• By resisting sin, we demonstrate that we are in a state of grace

â€¢ ••Righteousness Reigns••
	• Sin has no power over us when we resist it, and righteousness reigns instead (Rom. 6:14)
	• This means that we should not make provisions for the flesh to fulfill its sinful desires (Rom. 13:14)

â€¢ ••The Body of Sin••
	• The body of sin is a source of temptation and sin, but it can be overcome through spiritual resistance (Rom. 6:6)
	• When we resist the body of sin, it is "done away" and we are free from its power

â€¢ ••Sin Remains Until Death••
	• Sin and concupiscence remain in us until the end of our lives (Gal. 5:17, Rom. 7:19)
	• This is why we need to continually resist sin and seek spiritual growth

â€¢ ••The Need for Penance••
	• Resisting sin is not easy and requires many works of penance (Rom. 6:6)
	• Penance is necessary to prevent ease and idleness, which can lead to sin

â€¢ ••Baptism into Christ's Death••
	• When we are baptized into Jesus Christ, we are baptized into his death (Rom. 6:3)
	• This means that we are united with Christ in his death and resurrection, and we should seek to live in a way that is consistent with this new life

â€¢ ••The Savior's Death••
	• The Savior's death was a sacrifice for our double death (Rom. 6:3, Augustine's commentary)
	• Through Christ's death, we are freed from the power of sin and can live a new life in righteousness.

	• The soul must be mortified and made new before it can become capable of deeds of love.

	• A work is not good simply because it is done, but because it is done with the right intention.
• ••The Apostle's View••
	• The apostle agrees with the judgment that love is essential for fulfilling the divine commandments.
	• He argues that without love, even the most impressive knowledge and faith are worthless.
• ••Conclusion••

	• Love is the key to understanding and fulfilling the divine commandments.

	• The word addressed to the Romans is "how to accomplish [fulfill], I find not" (Romans 7:18), highlighting the contrast between the two.

	• The commandment to love God is impossible for us to fulfill on our own
	• We need faith and prayer to obtain what the law commands

â€¢ ••The Light of Nature vs. the Light of Grace••
	• The light of nature is compared to the light of grace, but is actually darkness
	• The light of nature is self-centered and seeks only its own good

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Light of Grace••
	• The light of grace sees God alone and seeks him above all else
	• It has a "right heart" and a "right spirit", seeking God's good above its own

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Light of Nature••
	• The light of nature sees only itself and seeks its own good above all else
	• It has a "perverse" and "wicked" heart, bypassing God and seeking only its own

â€¢ ••The Role of Faith and Prayer••

	• Love sets man free from sin and darkness, allowing him to seek God's good

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Faith and Love••
	• Faith and love are closely related, with faith being the foundation for love
	• Love is the fruit of faith, allowing us to seek God's good above our own

• ••Nature vs. Grace••
	• Nature is self-centered and values things only for its own benefit
	• Grace, on the other hand, seeks to glorify God in all things
• ••Idolatry of Nature••
	• Nature turns God into an idol and absolutizes itself
	• It values only what it can use for its own advantage and benefit
• ••Characteristics of Nature••
	• Happy and content only when it gets what it wants
	• Disturbed and disquieted when it doesn't get what it wants
• ••Characteristics of Grace••
	• Remains neutral and loves God's will in all things
	• Content with itself and others, regardless of circumstances
• ••The Law and Human Nature••
	• The law is weak because it is observed out of fear or self-interest
	• People outwardly observe the law, but inwardly they hate it
• ••The Problem with Human Nature••
	• Human nature is prone to evil and cannot fulfill the law's commands
	• Without grace, people are part of the "mass of perdition"
• ••The Role of Faith••
	• Faith in Christ through the Holy Spirit gives us the ability to love God's will
	• This faith enables us to perform acts and refrain from them for God's sake alone
• ••The Sign of True Faith••
	• We rejoice in God's will, even when it's difficult or sad
	• We praise and bless God at all times, regardless of circumstances
• ••The Limitations of Human Reason••

	• True love and obedience come from faith in Christ
• ••The Importance of Unrestrained Readiness to Love••
	• Only an unrestrained readiness to love can perform acts or refrain from them for God's sake alone

	• Hope changes the person who hopes into what they hope for
	• This transformation occurs because hope transfers the person into the unknown and hidden

â€¢ ••The Mystery of Hope••
	• The person who hopes does not know what they hope for, yet knows what they do not hope for
	• This paradox is a result of hope's ability to transfer the person into the unknown

â€¢ ••The Role of the Spirit••

	• The Spirit knows what we should pray for, even if we do not know ourselves

	• This is because God's counsel and will far exceed our own

	• God knows the thoughts of men and rejects their vain counsels

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Prayer••
	• When we pray to God, he may contravene all our conceptions and fulfill our prayers in unexpected ways
	• This is because God's wisdom and will are greater than ours

â€¢ ••The Value of Unanswered Prayers••
	• It is not a bad sign if the opposite of what we pray for appears to happen
	• This can be a good sign that God's will is being fulfilled in a way that exceeds our understanding.

â€¢ ••Predestination and Election••

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is not the cause of salvation, but rather the result of God's predestination and election (Rom. 8:29-30)
	• Those who are saved are saved by God's immutable love and will, not by their own merits (Rom. 8:37-39)

â€¢ ••The Certainty of Salvation••

â€¢ ••The apostle's experience of weakness and suffering••

	• The apostle must cut off the idea that he can achieve salvation through his own efforts
	• He must recognize that he is nothing in himself and can only find good in God

â€¢ ••The theologians' concept of "contingent"••
	• They argue that the elect are necessarily saved, but not by consequent necessity
	• This concept is irrelevant to the theme and is empty talk

â€¢ ••The distinction between "consequent necessity" and "necessity of the consequence"••
	• "Consequent necessity" refers to immutability and is attributed to God
	• "Necessity of the consequence" refers to the necessity determined by a cause that can no longer be changed

â€¢ ••Luther's critique of the theologians' concept••
	• He argues that the distinction between the two concepts is unreal and meaningless
	• He claims that the concept of "consequent contingency" is irrelevant to the theme

â€¢ ••The concept of "consequent contingency"••
	• It is a concept that is meant to understand or give occasion to understand the concept of salvation by decision
	• However, it is meaningless to ask whether the consequent is contingent, as only God is necessary in this sense

â€¢ ••The theologians' equivocation••
	• They twist the understanding by turning the necessity of an occurrence into the necessity of the essence of a thing
	• This equivocation is out of place in this context

â€¢ ••The question of necessity••
	• The question is about the necessity of the sequel, not the necessity of the essence of a thing
	• No one raises the question of whether a created thing is contingent in its being

â€¢ ••The conclusion••
	• The apostle must recognize that he is nothing in himself and can only find good in God
	• The theologians' concept of "contingent" is empty talk and irrelevant to the theme.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Necessity of Consequence••
	• The concept of necessity of consequence is discussed
	• It refers to the idea that an event will necessarily happen due to a chain of events
	• The question is raised whether a contingency can hinder this necessity

â€¢ ••The Absurdity of the Formula••
	• The formula "all things take place by necessity of consequence but not by consequent necessity" is considered absurd
	• It is argued that this formula amounts to saying that everything takes place by necessity, but the things that take place are not God himself

â€¢ ••The Nature of God's Action••
	• God's action is considered necessary if he wills it
	• However, the thing done is not in itself necessary, but rather contingent on God's will

â€¢ ••The Distinction between Necessity and Contingency••
	• The distinction between necessity and contingency is discussed
	• It is argued that contingency does not hinder the necessary occurrence of a sequence of events

â€¢ ••The Question of Contingency••
	• The question is raised whether contingency can hinder the necessity of a chain of events
	• It is argued that contingency does not hinder the necessary occurrence of a sequence of events

â€¢ ••The Concept of Predestination••
	• The concept of predestination is discussed
	• It is argued that God's predestination is immutable and certain

â€¢ ••The Relationship between God and Contingency••
	• The relationship between God and contingency is discussed
	• It is argued that with God, there is no contingency, but only with us

â€¢ ••The Nature of Time and the Essence of Things••
	• The nature of time and the essence of things are discussed
	• It is argued that both are in God's hands

â€¢ ••The Critique of Objections••
	• The objections and exceptions to the concept of predestination are discussed
	• It is argued that these objections are based on a misunderstanding of the concept of necessity and contingency

â€¢ ••The Comfort of Predestination••
	• The comfort of predestination is discussed
	• It is argued that predestination provides hope and comfort to those who are frightened by the concept of necessity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••I. Predestination in Scripture••

• The apostle illustrates predestination through:
	• The stories of Isaac and Ishmael, and Jacob and Esau (Rom. 9:10-13)
	• The contrast between the elect and the rejected (Rom. 9:14-18)
• The apostle quotes Scripture to support predestination:
	• "I will have mercy on whom I have mercy" (Rom. 9:15)
	• "For this purpose did I raise you up" (Rom. 9:17)

••II. Predestination in Jesus' Words••

• Jesus' words illustrate predestination:
	• "No one can snatch them out of the hand of my Father" (John 10:29)
	• "I speak not of you all; I know whom I have chosen" (John 13:18)
	• "No one can come to me, except the Father that sent me draw him" (John 6:44)

••III. Predestination in God's Works••

• God's works demonstrate predestination:
	• God's actions with Ishmael, Esau, Pharaoh, and the Egyptians (Rom. 9:17)
	• God's protection of his saints despite many evils (Rom. 9:18-23)
	• God's election of some to salvation despite their wickedness (e.g. Saul, Manasseh, Judas)

••IV. Objections to Predestination••

• The apostle addresses objections to predestination:
	• The objection that man has free will and can earn merits or demerits
	• The objection that predestination contradicts God's justice

••V. Response to Objections••

• The apostle responds to objections:
	• The power of free decision is enslaved to sin without grace (Rom. 9:20-21)
	• God's will is not subject to human judgment or argument (Rom. 9:20)
	• The potter has the right to shape the clay as he sees fit (Rom. 9:21)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Objection 1: Free Will••
	• The objection: Human free will is not truly free because it is influenced by sin.
	• The response: Human free will is free in respect to salvation, but not in respect to sin.

â€¢ ••Objection 2: God's Desire for Salvation••
	• The objection: God desires all men to be saved, but some are not.
	• The response: God's desire for salvation is limited to the elect, as stated in 2 Timothy 2:10.

â€¢ ••Objection 3: God's Condemnation of Sinners••
	• The objection: God condemns none who is without sin, but humans are necessarily in sin.
	• The response: Humans are in sin by their own will, not by coercion, and those who harden their hearts are given the will to sin.

â€¢ ••Objection 4: God's Commandments and Hardening of Hearts••
	• The objection: God issues commandments and then hardens men's wills to sin against them.
	• The response: God's commandments are for the elect to fulfill, and the reprobate are entangled in them to show God's wrath and mercy.

â€¢ ••The Prudence of the Flesh••
	• The prudence of the flesh seeks its own interests and fears its own misery more than God's glory.
	• This leads to a mind about God that is different from that of humans, who owe God nothing.

â€¢ ••God's Sovereignty••
	• God does not owe anyone anything, as stated in Job 41:11.
	• This is the word quoted in Romans 11:35, emphasizing God's sovereignty.

â€¢ ••The Bitter Taste of Predestination••
	• The prudence of the flesh is offended by predestination and may lead to blasphemy.
	• Those with the "prudence of the spirit" delight in predestination and are filled with happiness.

â€¢ ••The Response to Objections••
	• The apostle responds to objections by emphasizing God's sovereignty and election.
	• This response is meant to lead to a deeper understanding of God's ways and a greater trust in His goodness.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Understanding God's Ways••
	• The subject of predestination is bitter to the prudence of the flesh, but sweet to those with the "prudence of the spirit".
	• This understanding is essential for a deeper relationship with God and a greater trust in His sovereignty.

â€¢ ••The Call to Repentance••
	• The prudence of the flesh must be turned away from and replaced with the "prudence of the spirit".
	• This involves recognizing that salvation comes from outside oneself, in the election of God.

â€¢ ••The Elect and the Fear of God••
	• The elect are characterized by their humility and fear of God's word
	• They tremble at the thought of God's predestination, but this is a good sign

â€¢ ••Comfort in the Face of Fear••

â€¢ ••The Character of the Reprobate••
	• The reprobate do not tremble at God's word, but rather despise it
	• They may even say with despair: "If I am damned, all right, let me be damned"

â€¢ ••Ranks of the Elect••
	• There are three ranks of the elect:
		- Those who are content with God's will, whatever it is
		- Those who have resigned themselves to God's will, but still yearn for salvation
		- Those who have resigned themselves to hell, if God wills it

â€¢ ••The Highest Rank of the Elect••

	• This rank is the best and highest, as it indicates a complete surrender to God's will

â€¢ ••The Importance of Trust••
	• The elect must trust in God's truthfulness and accept His promise
	• This trust will free them from their former notion that God only frightens, and will save and elect them

â€¢ ••The Role of Anxiety••

	• The elect should rejoice in their anxiety and trust in God's truthfulness

â€¢ ••The Promise of God••
	• God cannot lie, and His promise is true
	• The elect can be confident in God's promise and accept it, even if it means accepting hell, if that is God's will.

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Remnant••
	• To be a seed for another people
	• To be a remnant of the true Israel
	• To be a witness to the Gentiles

â€¢ ••The Word of Faith••
	• The word of the Spirit, revealed to the apostle
	• Hidden and not easily comprehensible

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Unbelief••

â€¢ ••The Role of the Flesh••

	• To bring righteousness to those who believe
	• To cut off those who do not believe

â€¢ ••The Relationship between the Word and the Flesh••
	• The word of faith is cut off from the flesh and its wisdom

â€¢ ••The Outcome for Believers and Unbelievers••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of the Word••
	• The word is righteous, but only if it is bound to the invisible and not the visible.
	• The word is "cut short" in the sense that it is cut off from all that can be seen.
• ••The Imperfect Word••
	• The word that signifies something visible and created is imperfect and incomplete.
	• It only titillates a part of the person, their flesh and senses, and does not satisfy the whole person.
• ••The Consummated Word••
	• The word that proclaims the perfect and complete is consummated.
	• It satisfies the whole person and is directed towards the Spirit.
• ••The Relationship between the Word and the Flesh••
	• The word is cut short against the flesh, rejecting all that is good for the flesh.
	• The flesh is satisfied by temporal goods and promises, which is why the word of the letter is prolonged and extended.
• ••The Imperfect Word of the Letter••
	• The word of the letter is imperfect and dilated because it signifies something but does not actually display what it signifies.
	• It leads to wickedness, deceitfulness, and vanity.
• ••The Perfect Word of the Gospel••
	• The word of the gospel is complete because it actually makes available what it signifies, namely, grace.
	• It is abridged because it does not defer the actualization of what it announces.
• ••The Relationship between the Word and Faith••
	• The word of the gospel is "cut short in righteousness" because it avails righteousness to those for whom it is consummated and abridged.
	• This happens through faith.
• ••The Corollary••
	• The word of the old and new law is the same.
	• It can be called perfect or not perfect, short or long, only in terms of our understanding or lack of understanding.
• ••The Importance of Humility••
	• No one can receive the word of Christ unless they abjure everything else and rid themselves of their pride.
	• They must humbly surrender the sovereignty of their reason and their whole mind.
• ••The Consequences of Not Receiving the Word••
	• Most people persist in their pride and do not take hold of the word.
	• Hardly a remnant is saved, and the word is cut short in those who perish.

â€¢ ••Faith as the consummation of salvation••
	• Faith is the ultimate goal of salvation

â€¢ ••Faith as a separation from the world••

â€¢ ••Faith as righteousness••

â€¢ ••The word of the Spirit vs. the word of the dead letter••

	• It perfects the wisdom of the Spirit and destroys the wisdom of the flesh

â€¢ ••The word of the dead letter••
	• Consummates and hardens the proud and destroys the humble

	• Destroys Jerusalem and builds up Jericho

â€¢ ••The meaning of "consummated" and "cut short"••
	• "Consummated" refers to the terminus ad quem (the end goal)
	• "Cut short" refers to the terminus a quo (the starting point)
	• "Consummated" indicates perfection and righteousness, while "cut short" indicates separation from unrighteousness

â€¢ ••The word of faith as a coat or cover••
	• The word of faith is like a coat that is too short
	• It covers one but leaves the other naked
	• It requires one to fall out, namely, the "old man" in us

â€¢ ••The word of faith as a narrow path••
	• The word of faith is a narrow path that only one can walk
	• It requires one to leave behind the "old man" and walk in the "new man"

â€¢ ••The perfection and incompleteness of the word of faith••
	• The word of faith is perfect and consummated in its effects
	• It consummates man wonderfully and exceedingly well
	• However, it is short and incomplete in its ability to cover both the "old man" and the "new man"

â€¢ ••The two types of people who are left behind••
	• Those who want neither righteousness nor unrighteousness
	• Those who are left in the flesh and in unrighteousness

â€¢ ••The Consummation of God's Plan••
	• God will bring about a consummation, but it will be short and narrow.
	• This consummation will cut off the old man in most people, but leave a seed.

â€¢ ••The Abridgment of the Consummation••

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Consummation••

â€¢ ••The Stumbling Stone••
	• The people stumbled at the stumbling stone, which is Christ.
	• Those who believe in Christ will not be confounded, but will be secure in their conscience.

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Not Believing••

â€¢ ••The Punishment of the Damned••

â€¢ ••The State of the Evil Conscience••
	• An evil conscience that has forgotten Christ will be filled with horror and a desire to flee.

â€¢ ••The Fear of the Wicked••
	• The wicked are like the raging sea, which cannot rest.
	• They are like the dust that the wind drives away, and are terrified by the sound of a flying leaf.

â€¢ ••The Security of the Righteous••
	• Those who believe in Christ are secure in their conscience and are not frightened.
	• They are like a lion, bold and fearless, and are not anxious about what may happen to them.

â€¢ ••The Paradox of the Consummation••
	• The consummation is both short and narrow, yet overflowing with righteousness.
	• This is a paradox that is difficult to understand, but is a demonstration of God's power and wisdom.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Interpretation of "confundetur"••
	• The Septuagint translates it as "he shall not be confounded"
	• This means being incapable of orderly thought and planning due to fear and terror
	• The penalty of those who die outside of Christ is being perturbed and not knowing what to do

â€¢ ••Jerome's interpretation••
	• He translates it as "he does not hasten"
	• This means the Advent of the Lord does not seem near to him
	• This is in contrast to the Thessalonians who were upset and terrified at the thought of the Day of the Lord

â€¢ ••Alternative interpretation••
	• One who believes in Christ does not hasten with foolish zeal
	• They do not walk about judging others and excusing themselves
	• They are humble, teachable, and willing to be led

â€¢ ••The problem with excessive zeal••
	• Many people start with excessive zeal but end up being useless
	• They become disgusted with good efforts and have a lukewarm attitude
	• This is compared to the noonday devil who excites people to judge others

â€¢ ••The importance of humility••
	• One who believes in Christ is slow to speak and swift to hear
	• They are not quick to judge others but rather listen and learn
	• This is in contrast to the Jews who did not want to hear and hastened in their heart

â€¢ ••The meaning of "confundetur"••
	• It can also mean "he will be ashamed" or "he will blush with shame"
	• This is brought into accord with the idea of haste and shame

â€¢ ••The connection between shame and haste••
	• One who is ashamed dreads to show himself and wants to flee
	• This is compared to the saying "Fall upon us, mountains" (Hos. 10:8; Luke 23:30)

â€¢ ••The consequences of shame and haste••
	• This leads to confusion and a lack of orderly thought and planning
	• It causes people to flee and hide, rather than facing their fears and doubts

â€¢ ••The contrast with those who believe in Christ••
	• Those who believe in Christ are not ashamed or fearful
	• They are humble, teachable, and willing to be led

â€¢ ••The importance of understanding the text••
	• Luther does not fully understand the translation of "confundetur"
	• He notes that there are different interpretations and does not want to discuss it further

	• Man is always in a state of "not-being", "becoming", and "being"

â€¢ ••Repentance and Righteousness••

	• He is always repenting, always sinners, and always righteous

â€¢ ••The Human Condition••
	• Man is partly sinners and partly righteous

	• None is so good that he cannot become better, and none is so bad that he cannot become worse

â€¢ ••The Road to Heaven and Hell••
	• This life is a road to heaven and hell
	• Man's choices determine his path
	• He becomes what he is to be through his actions

â€¢ ••The Apostle's Teaching••
	• The apostle teaches that man is to be reformed by the renewing of his mind
	• This transformation is a renewing of the mind, day by day
	• It is a process of becoming greater and greater

â€¢ ••The True Sacrifice••
	• The true sacrifice to God is not outside us, but ourselves
	• We are to present our bodies as a living sacrifice, holy and acceptable to God
	• This sacrifice is our reasonable service

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Sacrifice••
	• The sacrifice is living, in contrast to the sacrifices of animals which were offered up dead
	• It is a spiritual sacrifice that brings forth good
	• The body of sin is dead before God, but good works done by the morally defiled are worth nothing

â€¢ ••The Importance of Good Works••
	• Good works done by the morally defiled are worth nothing
	• The body of sin must be done away with
	• The sacrifice is to bring forth good and overcome sin

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ••
	• Luther read the text with the understanding that it speaks of Christ
	• Christ is the eternal priest and the one who offers the sacrifice

â€¢ ••Scriptural References••
	• The text references various biblical passages, including Romans 3:11, II Corinthians 4:16, Ephesians 4:23, and Colossians 3:10.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Understanding the word "Holy"••
	• The word "holy" is often misunderstood to mean "firm" or "inviolable"
	• However, in the Scriptures, "holy" means "separate" and "set apart" from the profane
	• It signifies purity and cleanliness, especially in relation to God

â€¢ ••Etymology of "Holy"••
	• The word "holy" is derived from the Greek word "hagios", which means "sacred" or "pure"
	• Some etymologists have suggested that it comes from the Greek words "a" (without) and "ge" (earth), meaning "without earth" or "not of this world"

â€¢ ••Definition of "Holy"••
	• "Holy" means "clean", "chaste", and "separate" from the world
	• It signifies purity and cleanliness, especially in relation to God
	• It is not just about being neat or clean in human relations

â€¢ ••Examples of "Holy" in Scripture••
	• In Exodus 19:10, God tells the people to sanctify themselves before receiving the law
	• In Joshua 3:5, the people are told to sanctify themselves before crossing the Jordan River
	• In 1 Samuel 21:4, David says that the vessels of the young men are holy, meaning clean and chaste

â€¢ ••The apostle's understanding of "Holy"••
	• The apostle understands "holy" as "chaste" and "pure" in the sense that our bodies must not be defiled by dissipation
	• He says that our bodies must be presented to serve righteousness and sanctification

â€¢ ••The distinction between "Holy" and "Sacred"••
	• There is a confusion between the words "holy" and "sacred", as well as "sanctify" and "sanctification"
	• The apostle argues that "holy" means "pure and clean before God", while "sacred" refers to something that is set apart for God's use

â€¢ ••The importance of being acceptable to God••
	• The apostle argues that doing good and living cleanly are not enough if we take pride in them
	• We must be acceptable to God, not just to men

â€¢ ••The dangers of pride and vainglory••
	• Pride and vainglory can undermine our good works and make us foolish
	• They can make us seek to be acceptable to men rather than to God

â€¢ ••The call to serve God••
	• The apostle says that our reasonable service is to present our bodies to serve righteousness and sanctification
	• This means living a life that is pure, clean, and separate from the world

â€¢ ••The importance of humility••
	• We must be humble and unconcerned for the criticism of others
	• We must be willing to be displeased with ourselves and to seek to be acceptable to God alone.
