Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Simulated love••
	• People may pretend to love their neighbors, but in reality, they do not reprove or correct them when they see them committing mistakes or sins.
	• This kind of love is superficial and does not involve genuine concern for the neighbor's well-being.
• ••Types of simulated love••
	• Some people practice simulated love knowingly, admiring and praising the vices of others as if they were virtues.
	• Others practice simulated love unknowingly, being too quick to praise and slow to reprove, even when they encounter great vices.
• ••Neutralists••
	• Neutralists are people who speak or do neither good nor evil, often standing firm in their "elicited acts" but not taking effective action.
	• They may think they have done enough by wishing their neighbors well and rejecting evil, but this is not enough to demonstrate genuine love.
• ••The apostle's commandment••
	• The apostle commands us to "hate that which is evil, cleave to that which is good" (Rom. 12:9).
	• This commandment is not meant to be taken literally, but rather as a call to discernment and action.
• ••Discerning good and evil••
	• The apostle is not talking about external appearances, but rather about the inner workings of the human heart.
	• We must test all things and hold fast to that which is good, not just superficially, but with a deep understanding of what is truly good and evil.
• ••The two kinds of good and evil••
	• The good is twofold: invisible (God) and visible (good works and the exercise of virtues).
	• The evil is also twofold: invisible (the old man in us) and visible (sensuality and the neglect of the spirit).
• ••Relating to others••
	• When it comes to our neighbors, we must hate in them whatever is evil and cleave to that which is good, without fear or favor.
	• This requires us to take a stand and act, rather than just wishing or hoping for the best.
• ••The importance of action••
	• Genuine love requires us to take action, not just to feel or wish for something.
	• We must be willing to confront and correct our neighbors when they are doing evil, rather than just pretending to love them.
• ••The danger of dissimulation••
	• Dissimulation is a form of deception that can lead us to pretend to love our neighbors while actually doing nothing to help them.
	• We must be aware of this danger and strive to live out our love in a genuine and effective way.
• ••The call to discernment••
	• The apostle's commandment is a call to discernment, not just a simple command to hate or love.
	• We must learn to distinguish between true good and evil, and take action accordingly.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Hating Evil and Loving Good••
	• Hate the evil you are trying to avoid, but do not side with the evil of someone else
	• Align yourself with the good of someone else
	• Cherish and favor the good, but hate and hinder evil where you can

â€¢ ••The Church's State••
	• The church is in a miserable state because it does not hate the evil that can be found in it
	• This is not because the church members are evil, but because they do not hate evil

â€¢ ••Simulated Love••
	• Simulated love hates what is good and cleaves to what is evil
	• Simulated love makes people blind to what is good or evil

â€¢ ••Brotherly Love••

	• It is a love that is shown to those who are part of the household of faith

â€¢ ••Loving One Another••
	• Christians must love one another with a special kind of love
	• This love is characterized by tender affection and sympathy

â€¢ ••Honoring One Another••
	• Christians must honor one another with inward honor, which is represented by a high regard and esteem for one's fellow men
	• This honor is not simulated or mercenary, but is given freely

â€¢ ••Self-Denial and Humility••
	• Bestowing honor on someone else requires self-denial
	• You must judge yourself worthy to be put to shame, but regard others as worthy to be honored in preference to you

â€¢ ••The Nature of Love••
	• Love makes people blind to what is good or evil
	• Love can be simulated, but true love is characterized by tender affection and sympathy

â€¢ ••The Importance of Humility••
	• Humility is essential for true love and honor
	• You must count others better than yourself and regard them as worthy of honor in preference to you

â€¢ ••The Role of Love in the Church••
	• Love is essential for the health and well-being of the church
	• The church must be characterized by brotherly love and a willingness to honor one another.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Humility and Pride••
	• Humility precedes others in bestowing honor
	• Pride expects others to come before it

â€¢ ••Giving Honor••
	• We must give honor to one another without expecting anything in return

	• We must give honor to others without considering who comes first or last

â€¢ ••Diligence and Sloth••

â€¢ ••Love and Service••

	• We must give ourselves to others, not just our possessions

â€¢ ••Helping Others••

â€¢ ••Fervor and Ardor••

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Sloth••

â€¢ ••The Choice Between Spirit and Flesh••
	• We must choose to be fervent in spirit, not in the flesh

	• We must not let our fleshly desires lead us to laziness and boredom

â€¢ ••The Example of a Lazy Cook••
	• A lazy cook who serves food that gets cold is a bad example
	• We must not waste our work and our time by being lazy and half-hearted
	• We must strive to be diligent and fervent in our work

â€¢ ••The Importance of Fervor••
	• Fervor is essential for doing good works and serving others
	• We must be passionate about our work and not do it half-heartedly
	• Fervor leads to productivity and a sense of accomplishment.

â€¢ ••Serving the Lord••
	• Directed against those who serve their own interests or stubbornly persist in doing a good work when obedience calls them to something else

â€¢ ••Rejoicing in Hope••

â€¢ ••The Importance of Patience••

â€¢ ••The Problem with Vain Joy••

â€¢ ••The Importance of Heartfelt Prayer••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Dangers of Superficial Prayer••
	• We often pretend to pray without truly putting in the effort
	• This can lead to a false sense of security and peace
	• We may think we've prayed and are no longer troubled, but this is not true prayer

â€¢ ••The Call to Order and Vigilance••
	• The word "instant" is a call to constant effort in prayer
	• This is especially important for clergy, who must be vigilant in their prayer life
	• Prayer is a labor that requires a subdued and broken mind and a high and triumphant spirit

â€¢ ••The Two Kinds of Prayer••
	• Prayer can be vocal or mental
	• Vocal prayer is often accompanied by a "virtual intention" that can be a cover for laziness

â€¢ ••The Threefold Attentiveness of Vocal Prayer••
	• Material (or sensual) attentiveness: paying attention only to the words
	• This type of prayer is not true prayer, but rather an act of obedience
	• It can still be beneficial, but only in an extrinsic sense

â€¢ ••The Benefits of Vocal Prayer••
	• It can drive the devil away if spoken aloud in the spirit of simplicity
	• The divine word can affect the soul even if not understood
	• It offers the intellect and feeling an occasion they would not have otherwise

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Vocal Prayer••
	• People who pray in this way may not comprehend the feeling-tone of the words
	• This type of prayer is not true prayer, but rather a superficial imitation

â€¢ ••The Importance of Mental Prayer••
	• Mental prayer requires a more intense and personal effort
	• It involves a deeper engagement with the divine word and a more profound understanding of its meaning

â€¢ ••The Need for a Broken and Subdued Mind••
	• True prayer requires a mind that is broken and subdued
	• This is necessary for a high and triumphant spirit, which is essential for prayer

â€¢ ••The Role of the Clergy••
	• Clergy must be vigilant in their prayer life and strive for true prayer
	• They must not rely on superficial prayer, but rather seek to cultivate a deeper and more personal relationship with God

â€¢ ••The Labor of Prayer••
	• Prayer is a labor that requires effort and dedication
	• It is not a useless call, but rather a necessary one for those who seek to enter the priesthood or cultivate a deeper relationship with God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Critique of Endowments••
	• People make endowments for canonical hours with the expectation that prayers will be said for them.
	• They often prescribe the prayer, as if they can "buy" prayer.
	• This is seen as a misguided and selfish approach.

â€¢ ••Contrast with Ancestors••
	• The founders of monasteries and churches sought to glorify God, not just gain personal benefits.
	• Their endowments were made "for the salvation of our souls", not for personal gain.

â€¢ ••Questioning Motives••
	• Luther questions whether people's motives for making endowments are truly for God's glory.
	• He fears that some may be deceiving themselves and prioritizing personal gain.

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Romans 12:13••
	• The verse "Communicating to the necessities of the saints" is often misunderstood.
	• Luther argues that the "saints" referred to are not just the blessed, but all believers.

â€¢ ••Understanding the "Saints"••
	• The apostle Paul and Scripture use the term "saints" to refer to all believers, not just the blessed.
	• This understanding is often lost in modern times.

â€¢ ••Compassion and Helping the Saints••
	• The apostle Paul urges Christians to have compassion on the needs of the saints.
	• This is not just about providing material help, but also about showing sympathy and support.

â€¢ ••Helping the Persecuted••
	• The passage in Romans 12:13 can also be understood as referring to helping those who are persecuted.
	• This involves showing sympathy and support to those who are suffering for their faith.

â€¢ ••Modern Application••
	• Today, the "saints" may be those who are oppressed by want and shame, but keep their poverty secret.
	• These individuals can be thought of as taking the place of the martyrs of old.

â€¢ ••Temporal Necessities••
	• The needs of the saints referred to in Romans 12:13 are primarily temporal, not spiritual.
	• This involves providing material help and support to those in need.

â€¢ ••Spiritual Necessities••
	• Others may be in spiritual need, requiring prayer, encouragement, and support.
	• This involves showing compassion and sympathy to those who are struggling spiritually.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Showing Mercy••
	• The apostle's saying "He that shows mercy." refers to those who are spared suffering due to poverty.
	• Here, the apostle is concerned with those who must suffer despite not being poor.

â€¢ ••Hospitality••
	• The apostle commends hospitality to everyone, not just bishops.
	• Hospitality is a freely extended love for strangers, not a mercenary act.
	• The Greek word "philoxenia" means "love of strangers" or "loving to take care of strangers".

â€¢ ••Blessing Persecutors••
	• The apostle advises to bless those who persecute you, rather than cursing them.
	• This means rejoicing with those who rejoice and weeping with those who weep.

â€¢ ••Being of One Mind••
	• The apostle emphasizes the importance of being of one mind with others.
	• This means not minding high things, but rather being concerned with the humble.

â€¢ ••High and Humble Things••
	• The apostle speaks of "high" and "humble" things, not people.
	• He advises to take satisfaction in relating to the humble, rather than those in high positions.

â€¢ ••Augustine's Rule••
	• The apostle's advice is in line with Blessed Augustine's rule: "Do not glory in the high rank of wealthy parents but in the company you keep with your poor brethren".

â€¢ ••Translation Notes••
	• The Greek word "tapeinosis" means "humility" and "tapeinophrosyne" means "to be mindful of humble things".
	• "Tapeinosis" should be translated as "lowliness" to accurately convey the sense of the Latin.

â€¢ ••Humility••
	• Humility is characterized by being mindful of humble things and not despising what is lowly.
	• A person with humility is concerned with what is base and exposed to contempt.

â€¢ ••Regard for Humility••
	• The apostle notes that someone who regards the humility of others is an example of humility.
	• This is seen in the example of Mary, who regarded the humility of her handmaiden (Luke 1:48).

â€¢ ••Virtue of Humility••
	• Humility is a virtue that involves being mindful of humble things and accommodating oneself to what is inferior.
	• It is characterized by a concern for what is base and exposed to contempt.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Pleasing others••
	• The apostle Paul says to seek to please all men as far as possible (Romans 12:18)
	• Blessed Augustine says a good reputation is necessary for others, but a good conscience is sufficient for oneself

â€¢ ••Misusing Augustine's quote••
	• People often misquote Augustine to justify their own pride and contentiousness
	• They think Augustine is saying they should never take offense or let anyone disparage them

â€¢ ••Responding to insults••
	• If insulted, one should excuse oneself quietly and not fight back or acknowledge the insult
	• A woman who was beaten seven times bore the wrong without speaking up or hurting her reputation

â€¢ ••The liberty of conscience••
	• There is a shocking notion of the liberty of conscience, which is not the same as the liberty of intention
	• The higher clergy often grant dispensations based on "good intention" rather than actual behavior

â€¢ ••Abuse of power••
	• The lower clergy often obtain licenses to transfer or exchange ecclesiastical benefices based on "good intention"
	• They use clever pretexts to engage in fraudulent financial transactions

â€¢ ••Heaping coals of fire••
	• Blessed Augustine says we should induce a man who has done us harm to repent by showing him kindness
	• This is what is meant by the phrase "heaping coals of fire upon his head" (Romans 12:20)

â€¢ ••Converting others••
	• God converts people by showing them his goodness, not by threats or fear
	• A person converted by love is more self-critical and displeased with themselves than anyone else could be

â€¢ ••Benefactions for adversaries••
	• Benefactions performed for adversaries are the "coals of fire" that can convert them
	• These benefactions are not the same as those performed for friends, who are already entitled to receive benefits

â€¢ ••The nature of benefactions••
	• An enemy feels differently about a good deed than a friend does
	• An enemy is drawn to their benefactor because of their own unworthiness

â€¢ ••God's example••
	• God gave his only-begotten Son for us, showing us the power of love and kindness to convert others

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Christ as a furnace of fire••
	• Christ is a source of intense love and hatred
	• He is a furnace that burns away evil and wickedness
	• We should strive to be like Christ in our relationships with others

â€¢ ••Overcoming evil with good••
	• We should not respond to evil with evil, but with good
	• This means not becoming like our enemies, but rather changing them through our goodness
	• We should aim to make our enemies into good people who resemble us

â€¢ ••The true meaning of "overcoming evil"••
	• The phrase "overcome evil with good" is often misunderstood
	• It does not mean responding to evil with evil, but rather with something that highlights the evil's true nature
	• We should respond to evil with something that is distinct from it, not similar

â€¢ ••The danger of responding to evil with evil••
	• When we respond to evil with evil, we become like our enemies
	• We do not help them to see their folly, but rather make them more foolish
	• This approach only perpetuates the cycle of evil

â€¢ ••The importance of responding to evil with something distinct••
	• When we respond to evil with something that is distinct from it, we help the other person to see their folly
	• This approach can lead to change and transformation in the other person
	• It is a more effective way to overcome evil than responding with evil

â€¢ ••The role of silence in overcoming evil••
	• Sometimes, the best response to evil is to remain silent
	• This can be a sign of strength and wisdom, rather than weakness
	• It can also help to prevent further escalation of the conflict

â€¢ ••The importance of not becoming like our enemies••
	• We should strive to maintain our integrity and goodness in the face of evil
	• We should not let our enemies change us into something we are not
	• This is a key aspect of overcoming evil with good

â€¢ ••The role of comparison in overcoming evil••
	• When we compare evil with something that is distinct from it, we can see its true nature
	• This can help to highlight the evil's flaws and weaknesses
	• It can also help to lead to change and transformation in the other person

â€¢ ••The danger of enhancing evil through comparison••
	• If we compare evil with something that is similar to it, we may only make it more appealing
	• This can perpetuate the cycle of evil and make it harder to overcome
	• We should strive to compare evil with something that is distinct from it, not similar

â€¢ ••The importance of recognizing folly••
	• We should strive to recognize and acknowledge the folly of our enemies
	• This can help to lead to change and transformation in the other person
	• It is a key aspect of overcoming evil with good

â€¢ ••Submission to authority••

â€¢ ••The Accomplishment of Predecessors••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Secular powers vs. ecclesiastical powers••
	• Secular powers are stricter in punishing crimes like theft and murder
	• Ecclesiastical powers are more lenient and corruptible
• ••Ecclesiastical rulers' flaws••
	• They foster extravagance, greed, and strife
	• They punish those who seize church property, but not those who abuse their power
	• They promote unqualified and unfit individuals to high positions
• ••Double standard••
	• Ecclesiastical rulers judge others harshly, but are guilty of similar sins
	• They are more concerned with rebuking others than with their own behavior
• ••Lack of accountability••
	• Ecclesiastical rulers are not held accountable for their actions
	• They are more concerned with maintaining their power and status than with serving God
• ••Selective enforcement of laws••
	• Ecclesiastical rulers enforce laws that benefit themselves, but not those that would hold them accountable
	• They use their power to harass and punish others, while ignoring their own wrongdoing
• ••Prioritizing appearance over substance••
	• Ecclesiastical rulers focus on appearances and reputation, rather than true righteousness
	• They are more concerned with maintaining a good image than with living according to God's commandments
• ••Ignoring God's warning••
	• Ecclesiastical rulers ignore God's warning and guidance, and instead focus on punishing others
	• They are more concerned with maintaining their power and status than with serving God
• ••Hypocrisy••
	• Ecclesiastical rulers are hypocritical, judging others harshly while ignoring their own sins
	• They are more concerned with appearing righteous than with actually being righteous
• ••Need for reform••
	• The time will come when laws and practices like these will be reformed
	• The church needs to be reformed to prioritize true righteousness and service to God
• ••God's judgment••
	• God will judge the ecclesiastical rulers for their hypocrisy and abuse of power
	• They will be held accountable for their actions, and will face God's judgment.

â€¢ ••Luther's Critique of the Church••
	• Luther criticizes the Church for its corruption and extravagance
	• He quotes Blessed Bernard's sarcastic remark about bishops' priorities

â€¢ ••The Three-Part Nature of Man••
	• Luther explains that man is composed of body, soul, and spirit
	• The soul is the middle part, subject to the body and spirit

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Body, Soul, and Spirit••
	• The body is subject to political power, but only with the soul's consent
	• The spirit is lord over everything

â€¢ ••The Apostle's Teachings on Liberty and Servitude••
	• Luther cites several passages from the Apostle's letters that uphold liberty and reject servitude
	• Examples include Galatians 5:13, 1 Corinthians 6:12, and 1 Corinthians 7:23

â€¢ ••The Apostle's Use of Language••
	• Luther explains that the Apostle uses language that is relevant to his audience
	• He becomes "equivocal" when using words like "liberty" and "servitude"

â€¢ ••The Apostle's Address to Different Groups••
	• Luther notes that the Apostle addresses slaves and freemen in different terms
	• He commands slaves to obey their masters, but also encourages them to seek freedom

â€¢ ••The Apostle's Personal Experience••
	• Luther quotes the Apostle's statement in 1 Corinthians 9:19-22 about becoming all things to all men
	• The Apostle became a slave to gain more freedom and to save others

â€¢ ••The Importance of Context••
	• Luther emphasizes the importance of understanding the Apostle's teachings in their historical context
	• He notes that the Apostle's language and teachings must be interpreted in light of his audience and purpose

â€¢ ••Luther's Duty as a Teacher••
	• Luther explains that he must speak up against injustices and corruption, even in higher places
	• He feels compelled to perform his duty as a teacher, despite his distress

â€¢ ••The Need for Reform••
	• Luther concludes by emphasizing the need for reform in the Church
	• He calls for a return to the Apostle's teachings and a rejection of corruption and extravagance.

â€¢ ••The Commandment to Love Others as Yourself••

â€¢ ••The Importance of Applying the Standard••
	• People often break the commandment without realizing it

â€¢ ••Examples of Breaking the Commandment••

â€¢ ••The Need for Humility and Self-Reflection••

â€¢ ••The Importance of Meditating on the Commandment••
	• Moses taught the Children of Israel to meditate on the commandment and tell it to their children
	• This will help to internalize the commandment and guide one's actions.

â€¢ ••Three meanings of "sleep" in Scripture••

• ••Definition of Emulation••
	• Emulation is the desire to overcome evil with evil
	• It involves trying to do or say something worse than the other person

	• The word "emulation" comes from the Latin "aemulus" and "aemula", meaning "rival"
	• It is used as a noun or adjective to describe a person who is a rival or competitor

	• It involves hating the person who wants to share it and excluding them from it
• ••Types of Emulation••
	• Emulation can be loving envy or envious love (e.g. I Cor. 12:31)
	• It can also be a mental effort to surpass someone else without hating them (e.g. I Cor. 10:22)
• ••Emulation in Relationships••
	• A husband competes against an adulterer for his wife's love
	• A wife competes against an adulteress or mistress for her husband's love

	• Every place is sacred

	• Every garment is allowed

	• This would lead to a rejection of all good works, including those necessary for salvation
• ••The Importance of Good Works••
	• Good works, such as caring for widows, are necessary for salvation

• ••The Example of the Picards••

• ••The Rejection of the Picards' Heresy••

	• Good works, such as caring for the poor and the sick, are still necessary for salvation

â€¢ ••The Importance of Love in Keeping Vows••

	• Keeping a vow without love is equivalent to not keeping it at all

• ••The Dangers of Pride••
	• Those who are proud and self-satisfied try to avoid their fellow men and the weaknesses they represent.
	• This is a sign of foolishness and a lack of understanding of one's own dependence on others.
• ••The Importance of Humility and Recognition of Weakness••
	• We should recognize that we are all weak and prone to fall.
	• We should be humble and willing to suffer with others, rather than trying to avoid their weaknesses.
• ••The Role of God in Sustaining the Weak••
	• God has power to make the weak stand, but also to forsake them.
	• Many who stood firmly have fallen, and God's power is the only thing that can sustain us.
• ••The Call to Humility and Love••
	• We should listen to the apostle's call to humility and love.
	• We should recognize our own weaknesses and be willing to suffer with others, rather than trying to avoid their weaknesses.

	• The meaning of the saying is that the death and grave of Christ will be honored and celebrated throughout the world forever and ever

â€¢ ••Contradiction with the nature of death••
	• The death and grave of Christ contradict the nature of death, which normally drags away glory and fame
